Schubert line defects in 3d GLSM, part I: Complete flag manifolds and quantum Grothendieck polynomials
Cyril Closset, Wei Gu, Osama Khlaif, Eric Sharpe, Hao Zhang, Hao Zou

Abstract
We construct new half-BPS line defects in 3d 𝒩 = 2 \mathcal{N}=2 supersymmetric quiver gauge theories whose Higgs branches are complete flag manifolds X = Fl ⁡ ( n ) X={\rm Fl}(n) . Upon circle compactification, the bulk theory flows to a non-linear sigma model (NLSM) with target space X X and the line defects flow to objects supported on Schubert varieties X w ⊆ X X_{w}\subseteq X . These Schubert line defects form an important basis of the quantum K-theory of X X . They are realized as 𝒩 = 2 \mathcal{N}=2 supersymmetric quantum mechanics (SQM) quivers coupled to the 3d gauge theory. We show that the insertion of the Schubert line defect restricts the target space of the 3d gauged linear sigma model (GLSM) to the Schubert variety X w X_{w} , with the 1d degrees of freedom physically realizing a Bott–Samelson resolution of X w X_{w} . Moreover, we verify in examples that the 1d flavored Witten index of the quiver SQM reproduces the (equivariant) Chern character of the structure sheaf 𝒪 X w \mathcal{O}_{X_{w}} as a (double) quantum Grothendieck polynomial, generalizing previous results for X X a Grassmannian manifold. Our construction thus provides a more direct realization of the 3d GLSM/quantum K-theory correspondence for complete flag manifolds. Finally, in the small-circle limit, we obtain a 0d-2d coupled system that realizes the Schubert classes [ X w ] [X_{w}] in the quantum cohomology ring of X X .



1
Introduction

Two-dimensional non-linear sigma models (NLSM) onto Kähler manifolds
X
often arise as infrared (IR) limits of 2d
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetric gauge theories, also known as gauged linear sigma models (GLSM)
Witten:1993yc
. These models govern the so-called quantum geometry of
X
as probed by genus-zero string worldsheets
\Sigma
— in the NLSM, we have worldsheet instantons corresponding to non-trivial holomorphic maps
\Sigma\rightarrow X
, while in the GLSM we have a sum over gauge vortices
Morrison:1994fr
;
Closset:2015rna
;
Benini:2015noa
that can be formalized as quasimaps
ciocan2014stable
;
kim2014stablequasimapsholomorphicsymplectic
;
Kim:2016jye
. The relevant quantum geometry is encoded mathematically into the quantum cohomology ring
{\rm QH}^{\bullet}(X)
, which corresponds to the twisted chiral ring of the 2d GLSM.

In this work, we are interested in the 3d uplift, where we consider a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theory with gauge group
G
and with a Higgs branch given by the Geometric Invariant Theory (GIT) quotient

X\,\cong\,V\sslash G_{\mathbb{C}}\penalty\ ,

(1.1)

with the 3d Chern–Simons levels lying within windows such that there are no additional Coulomb or hybrid vacua
Intriligator:2013lca
;
Closset:2023jiq
;
Gu:2023hgm
;
Gu:2020zpg
. The 3d gauge theory on
\Sigma\times S^{1}
gives us an effective 2d
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetric description along
\Sigma
, which we call the 3d GLSM description. It naturally flows to an NLSM in 2d, but the presence of the additional circle direction modifies the IR interpretation significantly.

The twisted chiral operators are half-BPS line operators wrapping the circle direction — see
e.g.

Closset:2019hyt
.
Instead of the quantum cohomology ring
{\rm QH}^{\bullet}(X)
, for suitable Chern-Simons levels, the twisted chiral ring one obtains from those line operators in the 3d GLSM can be identified with the quantum K-theory ring of
X

(
Bullimore:2014awa
, section 2.4)
,
Jockers:2018sfl
;
Jockers:2019lwe
;
Jockers:2019wjh
;
Jockers:2021omw
;
Ueda:2019qhg
;
Koroteev:2017nab
;
Gu:2020zpg
;
Gu:2022yvj
;
Gu:2023tcv
;
Gu:2023fpw
;
Gu:2025abc
;
Gu:2021yek
;
Gu:2021beo
;
Dedushenko:2023qjq
;
Sharpe:2024ujm
;
Huq-Kuruvilla:2024tsg
;
Huq-Kuruvilla:2025nlf
.
One simple basis of half-BPS line operators are the Wilson lines for the 3d gauge group
G
, which flow to locally free sheaves over
X
. In a previous work, two of the authors considered the case of the Grassmannian manifold
X={\rm Gr}(k,n)\cong\mathbb{C}^{kn}\sslash{GL}(k)
and constructed half-BPS line defects that correspond to more general coherent sheaves
Closset:2023bdr
, namely the Schubert classes with compact support on Schubert varieties inside
X
. It was also shown that these line defects provide a physical construction of the so-called Grothendieck polynomials that can be used to represent the Schubert varieties in the quantum K-theory ring of
X
. This construction was later generalized to Lagrangian Grassmannians by a complementary subset of the authors
Gu:2025tda
.

In this work and its sequel
Closset:2025pfm
, we generalize the line defect construction of Schubert classes
Closset:2023bdr
;
Gu:2025tda
to flag manifolds. Recall that a flag

V_{\bullet}\,=\,(V_{1}\,\subset\,V_{2}\,\subset\,\cdots\,\subset\,V_{s}\,\subset\,\mathbb{C}^{n})

(1.2)

consists of nested linear subsets
V_{i}\,\cong\,\mathbb{C}^{{\rm dim}(V_{i})}
of some fixed ambient linear space
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The complete flag manifold is defined as the set of all complete flags:

{\rm Fl}(n)\,=\,\{V_{\bullet}=(0\,\subset\,V_{1}\,\subset\,V_{2}\,\subset\,\cdots\,\subset\,V_{n-1}\,\subset\,\mathbb{C}^{n})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \dim(V_{i})\,=\,i\}\penalty\ ,

(1.3)

and the partial flag manifold consists of all partial flags with fixed dimension vector
{\bf k}=(k_{\ell})
:

{\rm Fl}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{s};n)\,=\,\{V_{\bullet}\,=\,(0\,\subset\,V_{1}\,\subset\,V_{2}\,\subset\,\cdots\,\subset\,V_{s}\,\subset\,\mathbb{C}^{n})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \dim(V_{\ell})\,=\,k_{\ell}\}\penalty\ .

(1.4)

Obviously, this generalizes the Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}(k,n)={\rm Fl}(k;n)
. A famous property of flag manifolds is that they admit a decomposition as a disjoint union of Schubert cells:

{\rm Fl}({\bf k};n)\,=\,\bigsqcup_{w\in{\rm W}^{({\bf k};n)}}\,X_{w}^{\circ}\penalty\ .

(1.5)

While the indexing set
{\rm W}^{({\bf k};n)}
appearing here will be defined in the companion paper
Closset:2025pfm
, the Schubert cells of the complete flag are simply indexed by permutations
w\in S_{n}
:

{\rm Fl}(n)\,=\,\bigsqcup_{w\in S_{n}}\,X_{w}^{\circ}\penalty\ .

(1.6)

The Schubert variety
X_{w}
is the closure of the Schubert cell,
X_{w}=\overline{X_{w}^{\circ}}
. Note that there are
n!
distinct Schubert varieties inside
X={\rm Fl}(n)
, including
X
itself.

Schubert line defects for complete flag manifolds.
Let us focus on the complete flag manifold,
X={\rm Fl}(n)
, which is the subject of this paper. (More general partial flag manifolds will be discussed in the sequel
Closset:2025pfm
.) The key result of this paper is an explicit construction of a 1d-3d coupled system which defines a half-BPS defect line
{\mathscr{L}}_{w}
that will flow to
an object supported on
the Schubert variety
X_{w}
.
The defect line
{\mathscr{L}}_{w}
is defined as an
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric quantum mechanics (SQM)
Witten:1982im
— more specifically, it is a 1d quiver gauge theory, with 1d unitary gauge group
G_{\rm 1d}=\prod_{\ell}U(r_{\ell})
and a specific matter content determined by
w\in S_{n}
, coupled to the 3d bulk fields. Our key result is that, when specialized to the location of the line insertion at a point on
\Sigma
, the 1d-3d coupled system behaves as a 1d GLSM for the Schubert variety
X_{w}
itself. Indeed, our construction is closely related to a recent mathematical description of the Bott–Samelson resolution
BottSamelsonResolution
;
DemazureOnBottSamelson
of Schubert varieties using quiver subrepresentations
iezzi2025quivergrassmanniansbottsamelsonresolution
, or equivalently in terms of bioriented flags
cibotaru2020bioriented
. Physically, the additional one-dimensional degrees of freedom on the line provide us with the resolution of the Schubert variety.

The 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theory couples to the 3d gauge fields and superpartners as well as to
GL(n)
fugacities
1
1

1

We keep the
GL(n)
manifest in our notation, but of course the global symmetry is only
SL(n)
.

, realizing a 3d symmetry-breaking pattern specified by
w\in S_{n}
. Taking the flavored Witten index at fixed 3d gauge fugacities
x^{(i)}
, we obtain a polynomial in the 3d gauge fugacities
x^{(i)}
as well as in the
T\equiv(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}\subset{GL}(n)
fugacities
y_{i}
. Our second key result is that the Witten index of the 1d lines precisely reproduces the (double) quantum Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(q)}(x,y)
that are known to represent the (equivariant) Schubert classes in the quantum K-theory of the complete flag manifold
Buch02
;
FL94
;
lascoux1982structure
;
Las90
;
lenart2006quantum
;
MNS23
. We find:

{\bf I}_{\rm W}({\mathscr{L}}_{w})\,\cong\,{\rm ch}_{T}([\mathcal{O}_{w}])\,=\,\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(q)}(x,y)\penalty\ ,

(1.7)

where the second equality holds in the twisted chiral ring of the 3d GLSM. Indeed, the Witten index is naturally computed in terms of
n(n-1)/2
variables
x^{(i)}_{a_{i}}
(
a_{i}=1,\cdots,i
for
i=1,\cdots,n-1
), which correspond mathematically to exponentiated (K-theoretic) Chern roots for the tautological vector bundles
\mathcal{S}_{i}
over
{\rm Fl}(n)
with fiber
V_{i}
. Instead, the quantum K-theory ring relations and the quantum Grothendieck polynomials themselves are usually expressed in terms of
n
variables
x_{i}
corresponding to the quotient line bundles
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
with fiber
V_{i}/V_{i-1}

lenart2006quantum
. One can systematically transform any polynomial in the
x^{(i)}
variables into one in the
x_{i}
variables using the quantum K-theory relations, which leads us to the advertised result. More precisely, in this paper, we check (
1.7
) explicitly for
n\leq 4
; a general proof of the identity is left for future work, though it is clear from the physics that it should hold in general.
(For the special case of the longest permutation, for general
n
, our proposal matches the result in
(
ahkmox
, Theorem 1.2)
.)

Plan of the paper.
The paper is structured as follows. In section
2
, we provide general comments on coupling 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories to 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories, and emphasize the role of 1d dualities in the description of the line defects. In section
3
, we give a brief review of the GLSM construction of complete flag manifolds. We state the quantum cohomology ring relations as well as the quantum K-theory ring relations.
Section
4
discusses Schubert varieties and presents our construction of the Schubert line defects in the 3d GLSM. We provide a detailed discussion on the way these defects restrict the target space to the corresponding Schubert variety and how the 1d degrees of freedom realize a resolution thereof. We also compute the flavored Witten indices for 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM on the defects. We check in an explicit example the matching between these indices and the expected Chern characters of the Schubert classes in the quantum K-theory ring of Fl
(3)
. To complement this section, we also added the details of the calculations for the Fl
(4)
case in appendix
C
.
Taking a strict 2d limit of the 3d GLSM, in section
5
we discuss the resulting 0d-2d coupled system. We show in examples that the
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric matrix model realizes the Schubert classes in the equivariant quantum cohomology ring of the flag manifold.
In appendix
A
, we give a review of the definitions of the (quantum) Schubert and Grothendieck polynomials and discuss their geometric significance to our work. In appendix
B
, we provide some technical details relating the quiver construction on the defects to quiver Grassmannians and Bott-Samelson resolutions.

A comment on conventions.

In this paper, we denote permutations using the ‘window’ notation,
as in e.g.
Billey-Lakshmibai-2000
, in which the
i
-th entry is the image of
i
.
For example,
(3\,2\,4\,1)
denotes the permutation that sends

1\,\mapsto\,3,\>\>\>2\,\mapsto\,2,\>\>\>3\,\mapsto\,4,\>\>\>4\,\mapsto\,1.

This should be distinguished from the ‘cycle’ notation, in which
(3\,2\,4\,1)
denotes the permutation that maps

3\,\,\mapsto\,\,2\,\,\mapsto\,\,4\,\,\mapsto\,\,1\,\,\mapsto\,\,3.

To take another example, the identity permutation in window notation is
(1\,2\,3\,4)
, whereas in cycle notation it is
(1)\,(2)\,(3)\,(4)
.

2
Defect line operators of 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
quivers as 1d–3d coupled quivers

In this section, we discuss general aspects of defect line operators in 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theories. We first discuss the coupling of 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM to 3d gauge fields in some generality, and then specialize to the case of unitary quiver SQM.

2.1
Twisted chiral ring of half-BPS lines and 1d-3d coupled defects

The half-BPS lines of 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric field theories form an algebra
\mathcal{R}_{\rm 3d}
under fusion of parallel lines. Given any set of lines
\{{\mathscr{L}}_{i}\}
forming a basis of
\mathcal{R}_{\rm 3d}
, the fusion ring takes the form (see
e.g.

Witten:1993xi
;
Nekrasov:2009uh
;
Closset:2016arn
):

{\mathscr{L}}_{i}\,\star\,{\mathscr{L}}_{j}\,=\,{\mathcal{N}_{ij}}^{k}(y,q)\,{\mathscr{L}}_{k}\penalty\ ,\qquad\quad{\mathcal{N}_{ij}}^{k}(y)\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}(y,q)\penalty\ .

(2.1)

The fusion coefficients
{\mathcal{N}_{ij}}^{k}
generally depend on the flavor fugacities
y
and
q
, where
y
are ordinary flavor parameters while
q
are parameters for topological symmetries (FI parameters) which give us the ‘quantum parameters’ of the 3d ring in the geometric phase. This ring structure is best understood by considering the 3d theory on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{1}_{\beta}
and wrapping the lines along the circle
S^{1}_{\beta}
. Then, the operators
{\mathscr{L}}_{i}
are interpreted as local operators in the 2d effective description along
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The line operators are twisted chiral operators,
i.e.
they commute with the two supercharges
Q_{-}
and
\overline{Q}_{+}
of the 2d
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetry algebra; as per usual, the twisted chiral ring (
2.1
) is obtained in (joint) cohomology of these two supercharges.

A 3d GLSM is defined to be a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theory with a Higgs phase, compactified on
S^{1}_{\beta}
. For simplicity, we assume that the Higgs branch is the only moduli space of vacua of the 3d theory —
i.e.
that there are no additional Coulomb or hybrid vacua. The circle compactification allows us to understand the infrared dynamics in two-dimensional terms, as an NLSM with target space
X
being the Higgs branch of the 3d gauge theory. The main effect of the circle compactification is that supersymmetric ground states correspond to the quantum K-theory of
X
, instead of the standard quantum cohomology in a 2d NLSM. In short, one identifies the twisted chiral ring geometrically, in 2d or 3d, respectively, as:

\mathcal{R}_{\rm 2d}\,\cong\,{\rm QH}^{\bullet}(X)\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad\mathcal{R}_{\rm 3d}\,\cong\,{\rm QK}(X)\penalty\ .

(2.2)

In the 3d GLSM picture, the key question is to identify the quantum K-theory class to which a specific half-BPS line will flow in the infrared:

{\mathscr{L}}_{i}\qquad\rightsquigarrow\qquad[\mathcal{E}_{i}]\,\in\,{\rm QK}(X)\penalty\ .

(2.3)

Here
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is a specific coherent sheaf over
X
or, more generally, an element of the derived category of coherent sheaves of
X
— in (quantum, equivariant) K-theory, this amounts to a formal linear combination of coherent sheaves (with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}(q,y)
).

Let us consider a 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theory with gauge group
G
and chiral multiplets transforming in some linear representation
\mathfrak{R}\cong\mathbb{C}^{\text{dim}(\mathfrak{R})}
of
G
. The Higgs branch is then given, schematically, by a GIT quotient:

X\,\cong\,[\mathfrak{R}\sslash G_{\mathbb{C}}]\penalty\ .

(2.4)

Note that
X
has complex dimension

\text{dim}(X)\,=\,\text{dim}(\mathfrak{R})\,-\,\text{dim}(G_{\mathbb{C}})\penalty\ .

(2.5)

For instance, in the classic example of the complex Grassmannian
X={\rm Gr}(k,n)
, we take
G=U(k)
and
\mathfrak{R}\cong\mathbb{C}^{kn}
(
n
copies of the fundamental representation), hence
\text{dim}(X)=k(n-k)
.

Wilson lines as locally free sheaves.
A typical half-BPS line operator in the 3d gauge theory is the Wilson loop in some representation
R
of
G

Closset:2019hyt
:

W_{R}\,=\,\text{Tr}_{R}\,P\exp{\left(-i\int_{S^{1}_{\beta}}\left(A-i\sigma d\tau\right)\right)}\penalty\ .

(2.6)

In the geometric phase, this flows to the homogeneous vector bundle:

\mathcal{E}_{R}\,\equiv\,(\mathfrak{R}\times R)\sslash G_{\mathbb{C}}\,\longrightarrow\,X\penalty\ ,

(2.7)

where
G_{\mathbb{C}}
acts on
R
in the corresponding representation.
In particular, the Chern character of
\mathcal{E}_{R}
can be directly recovered from (
2.6
).

Vortex lines as coherent sheaves.
A general
vortex line
or, more generally,
defect line
, can be defined as a 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theory coupled to the 3d bulk fields --- this is a standard way of constraining the 3d gauge and matter fields at the location of the line, thus defining a line defect after integrating out the worldline degrees of freedom.
2
2

2

For a more recent discussion in the 3d holomorphic-topological (HT) twist framework, see
Dimofte:2025oqf
.
We refer to the recent work
Closset:2025zyl
, whose conventions we follow, for a general discussion of 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories.

The 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM consists of a 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet for some 1d gauge group
G_{\rm 1d}
, coupled to 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
chiral multiplets
\varphi
in some representation
R_{\varphi}
of
G_{\rm 1d}
and some 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
Fermi multiplets
\Gamma
in some representation
R_{\Gamma}
of
G_{\rm 1d}
. The 1d chiral and Fermi multiplets also transform non-trivially under the 3d gauge field
G
, which enters as a flavor symmetry from the worldline perspective. Finally, we have the
E
- and
J
-terms that impose relations among chiral multiplets:

E_{\Gamma}(\varphi,\phi)\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\qquad J_{\Gamma}(\varphi,\phi)\,=\,0\penalty\ .

(2.8)

Note that they also couple together the 1d chiral multiplets
\varphi
and the 3d chiral multiplets
\phi
.
Here, for definiteness, let us assume that
J=0
while we have a non-trivial
E
-term for each Fermi field
\Gamma
— this will be the case considered in this work. In appropriate circumstances, the defect line defines a coherent sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
which is simply the structure sheaf of a subvariety:

Y\,\cong\,\left(\mathfrak{R}\,\times\,R_{\varphi}\,|\,E_{\Gamma}\,=\,0\right)\sslash G_{\mathbb{C}}\,\times\,(G_{\rm 1d})_{\mathbb{C}}\penalty\ .

(2.9)

Namely, one constructs the zero locus of the section
E_{\Gamma}(\varphi,\phi)
inside
\mathfrak{R}\times R_{\varphi}
before taking a GIT quotient by both the 1d and 3d gauge groups. The resulting coherent sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
over
X
has support over the subvariety
Y
of dimension:

\text{dim}(Y)\,=\,\text{dim}(\mathfrak{R})\,+\,\text{dim}(R_{\varphi})\,-\,\text{dim}(R_{\Gamma})\,-\,\text{dim}(G_{\mathbb{C}})\,-\,\text{dim}((G_{\rm 1d})_{\mathbb{C}})\penalty\ .

(2.10)

This schematic description involves a number of subtleties which we shall not fully discuss, including the fact that the 1d Fermi multiplets must be in a phase of the
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM where they are all massive, and that we implicitly assumed specific choices of 1d Chern–Simons levels
Closset:2025zyl
. Our goal here is not to give a rigorous theory of 1d vortex lines in 3d GLSMs, but only to motivate the explicit construction of this paper in the special case of flag manifolds. Thus, let us simply illustrate the general discussion above with a very simple example.

Examples of defect lines on
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}={\rm Fl}(2)
.
Consider
G=U(1)
coupled to two chiral multiplets
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
of unit charge. For a positive FI parameter,
\zeta>0
, the Higgs branch is the projective space:

X\,=\,\mathbb{C}^{2}\sslash{GL}(1)\,\cong\,{\rm Fl}(2)\,=\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\penalty\ ,

(2.11)

with homogeneous coordinates
[\phi_{1},\phi_{2}]
. First, consider a Wilson loop of charge
p\in\mathbb{Z}
,

W_{p}\,=\,e^{-ip\int_{S^{1}_{\beta}}(A-i\sigma d\tau)}\,\cong\,x^{p}\,=\,{\rm ch}(\mathcal{O}(-p))\penalty\ ,

(2.12)

where
x
it the
U(1)
gauge fugacity. At the location of the vortex line itself, we can interpret the charge
p
as a 1d Chern–Simons level, so that the 1d ground states built out of the fields
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
are precisely sections of the line bundle
Hori:2014tda
;
Closset:2025zyl
:

\mathcal{O}(-p)\,\cong\,(\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C})\sslash{GL}(1)\penalty\ ,

(2.13)

as expected from (
2.7
). Next, we would like to engineer a skyscraper sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{p}
with support at the point
p=[a,b]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. This is easily achieved by introducing a single 1d chiral multiplet
\varphi
of charge
1
under
G_{\rm 1d}=U(1)
(with 1d bare CS level
Q=0
) and of charge
-1
under
G=U(1)
. We also introduce a single Fermi multiplet
\Gamma
of charge
1
under
G_{\rm 1d}
and which couples to the 1d and 3d fields by the
E
-term constraint:

E_{\Gamma}\,=\,\varphi\,(b\,\phi_{1}\,-\,a\,\phi_{2})\,=\,0\penalty\ .

(2.14)

Finally, we need to pick the 1d FI parameter
\xi>0
. Then, the 1d and 1d-3d
D
-term equations on the worldline read:

|\varphi|^{2}\,=\,\xi\penalty\ ,\qquad-|\varphi|^{2}\,+\,|\phi_{1}|^{2}\,+\,|\phi_{2}|^{2}\,=\,\zeta\penalty\ .

(2.15)

We are forced to have
\varphi\neq 0
, which fully Higgses
G_{\rm 1d}
, and the
E
-term constraint then gives us
b\phi_{1}-a\phi_{2}=0
. Up to gauge transformations, this fixes

[\phi_{1},\phi_{2}]\,=\,[a,b]\penalty\ ,

(2.16)

which is the expected support of the skyscraper sheaf. Indeed, we simply gave a physics construction to the resolution:

0\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{O}(-1)\,\overset{E_{\Gamma}}{\longrightarrow}\,\mathcal{O}\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{O}_{p}\,\longrightarrow\,0\penalty\ ,

(2.17)

as discussed in
Closset:2023bdr
. Finally, one can check that the Witten index of the 1d gauge theory reads:

{\bf I}_{\rm W}\,=\,-\oint_{(z=x)}{dz\over 2\pi iz}\,{1\,-\,z\over 1\,-\,z\,x^{-1}}\,=\,1\,-\,x\,=\,{\rm ch}(\mathcal{O}_{p})\penalty\ ,

(2.18)

as expected from (
2.17
).

2.2
Unitary quivers and dualities on the worldline

Let us now specialize to the kind of 1d-3d coupled systems considered in this paper. Both the 3d and the 1d gauge groups are taken to be products of unitary gauge groups:

G\,=\,\prod_{i}\,U(k_{i})\penalty\ ,\qquad\quad G_{\rm 1d}\,=\,\prod_{\ell}\,U({\rm r}_{\ell})\penalty\ .

(2.19)

The 3d chiral multiplets
\phi
are in bifundamental or (anti)-fundamental representations of the
U(k_{i})
’s, corresponding to a 3d quiver, and similarly for the 1d chiral multiplets
\varphi
, so that the 1d worldline theory is embedded into a larger quiver with gauge group
G\times G_{\rm 1d}
— we may also include flavor nodes, denoted by squares.
Finally, we introduce bifundamental Fermi multiplets
\Gamma
, with corresponding
E
-terms constraints:

E_{\Gamma}(\varphi,\phi)\,=\,0\penalty\ ,

(2.20)

which impose relations among the 1d-3d chiral-multiplet quiver arrows.
A simple example, corresponding to the
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
discussed above, is shown in figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
A simple example: the 3d quiver for
X={\rm Fl}(2)=\mathbb{P}^{1}
coupled to a 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM engineering the skyscraper sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{p}
. The 1d quiver is indicated by the dashed blue rectangle. Note that the Fermi multiplet
\Gamma
is not in the antifundamental of the
SU(2)
global symmetry; instead, we have a single
\Gamma
fields which breaks the flavor symmetry explicitly through the
E
-term (
2.14
).

As part of the definition of the 1d defect, one needs to specify the 1d FI parameters
\xi_{\ell}
; they are chosen so that the 1d-3d quiver admits a non-trivial geometric phase. One also needs to specify the bare 1d Chern–Simons levels
Q_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}
, in the conventions of
Closset:2025zyl
. In this work, for simplicity, we choose

Q_{\ell}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\qquad\xi_{\ell}\,>\,0\penalty\ ,

(2.21)

for every
U({\rm r}_{\ell})
gauge group.
It is not difficult to relax this constraint, but this will not be necessary for our purpose.

Dualities in 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM.
The 1d unitary quivers of the type discussed here admit Seiberg-like dualities recently discussed in
Closset:2025zyl
. Here, we present some simple consequences of these dualities for our description of 1d defect lines. Indeed, using 1d dualities, complicated 1d quivers can often be simplified. Here we will focus on duality moves that correspond to the ‘full confinement’ of a 1d unitary gauge node, effectively removing the node and replacing it with some effective ‘mesonic’ arrows.

These duality operations are local on the quiver. Consider a
U({\rm r})
node connected to other nodes (gauge or flavor) by (anti)fundamental chiral and Fermi arrows, as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm r}
\displaystyle n_{2}
\displaystyle n_{1}
\displaystyle n_{4}
\displaystyle n_{3}

(2.22)

Here,
n_{1}
and
n_{3}
denote the total number of fundamental chiral and Fermi multiplets, respectively, while
n_{2}
and
n_{4}
denote the total number of antifundamental chiral and Fermi multiplets, respectively.
3
3

3

Hence, for instance, the node denoted by
n_{2}
might not be a
U(n_{2})
node, because it could be split into several
U(m_{i})
nodes with
\sum_{i}m_{i}=n_{2}
, and similarly for the other nodes surrounding
U({\rm r})
in (
2.22
).
In the following discussion, the condition (
2.21
) is crucial. We then find three useful duality moves, as a direct application of the results of
Closset:2025zyl
.

Type I duality.
The simplest confinement duality occurs if and only if
n_{1}={\rm r}
, in which case we have the following confinement duality:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}

(2.23)

In this work, we call this duality move a ‘Type I’ duality. In the language of the 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
Seiberg-like dualities
Closset:2025zyl
, this is a right-mutation at the
U({\rm r})
node.

Type II and Type III dualities.

The second and third duality moves, dubbed ‘Type II’ and ‘Type III’ dualities in the following, are more intricate and require one of the following two sets of conditions:

\displaystyle\text{Type II:}\qquad

\displaystyle n_{4}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle r}\,=\,n_{1}\,-\,n_{3}\penalty\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle r}\,\geq\,n_{2}\penalty\ ,

(2.24)

\displaystyle\text{Type III:}\qquad

\displaystyle n_{4}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle r}\,=\,n_{2}\penalty\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle r}\,\leq\,n_{1}\,-\,n_{3}\penalty\ .

If either of these conditions holds, we have the confinement duality:

(2.25)

In the language of Seiberg-like dualities, this is a right mutation followed by a trivial wall-crossing plus another right mutation (Type II) or a trivial wall-crossing followed by a left mutation (Type III)
Closset:2025zyl
.
These three 1d duality moves often allow us to simplify the rectangular quivers significantly, to be introduced below, by removing ‘redundant’ nodes.

3
Review of GLSMs for complete flag manifolds

Figure 2
:
The 2d quiver gauge theory of interest. The circle nodes are gauge groups
U(i)
, and the square node denotes the flavor symmetry group
SU(n)
.
The fields
\phi_{i}^{i+1}
are bifundamental scalars in chiral multiplets. The Higgs branch of this theory is the complete flag variety Fl
(n)
.

In this section, we review the GLSM construction of the complete flag manifold Fl
(n)
:

{\rm Fl}(n)\,=\,\{V_{\bullet}=(0\subset V_{1}\subset V_{2}\subset\cdots\subset V_{n-1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \dim(V_{i})=i\}\penalty\ .

(3.1)

In the first subsection, we review the classical and quantum cohomology relations for this manifold from the 2d GLSM perspective. In the second subsection, we consider the 3d (K-theoretic) uplift of the 2d theory, which gives us access to the quantum K-theory of Fl
(n)
.

3.1
2d GLSMs and quantum cohomology of complete flag manifolds

The complete flag manifold Fl
(n)
can be realized as the target space of a 2d GLSM by considering the 2d
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
quiver gauge theory shown in figure
2
—
see
Witten:1993xi
;
Donagi:2007hi
. We have the gauge group:

G\,=\,U(1)\,\times\,U(2)\,\times\,\cdots\,\times U(n-1)\penalty\ .

(3.2)

Associated with each
U(i)
gauge group we have the gauge vector multiplet
\mathcal{V}^{(i)}=(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)},\cdots)
, where
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}
is a complex adjoint scalar whose
i
eigenvalues we denote by
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{a_{i}}
. Moreover, between the quiver nodes
U(i)
and
U(i+1)
, we have the 2d chiral multiplet
\Phi_{i}^{i+1}=(\phi^{i+1}_{i},\cdots)
in the bifundamental representation
(\square_{i},\overline{\square}_{i+1})
.

Looking at the
D
-term equations of this theory, one can show that the complete flag manifold Fl
(n)
is the Higgs branch of the theory, which can be written as the GIT quotient:

{\rm Fl}(n)\,=\,\left(\bigtimes_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{C}^{i(i+1)}\right)\sslash G_{\mathbb{C}}\penalty\ .

(3.3)

Here,

G_{\mathbb{C}}={GL}(1)\,\times\,\cdots\,\times\,{GL}(n-1)
is the complexified gauge group. The complex dimension and topological Euler characteristic of this manifold are given by:

\dim({\rm Fl}(n))\,=\,\frac{n\,(\,n\,-\,1\,)}{2}\penalty\ ,\qquad\quad\chi({\rm Fl}(n))\,=\,n!\penalty\ .

(3.4)

Universal bundles and quantum cohomology.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{S}_{i}
the tautological vector bundle over Fl
(n)
whose fiber is the vector space
V_{i}
in (
3.1
). The eigenvalues
\widetilde{\sigma}_{a_{i}}^{(i)}
introduced above can be interpreted as the Chern roots of
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. The complete flag manifold also comes equipped with the universal quotient line bundles
\{\mathcal{Q}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{Q}_{n}\}
such that:

\mathcal{Q}_{i}\,\equiv\,\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}_{1}\penalty\ ,\qquad&i=1\penalty\ ,\\
\mathcal{S}_{i}/\mathcal{S}_{i-1}\penalty\ ,\qquad&i=2,\cdots,n-1\penalty\ ,\\
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\mathcal{S}_{n-1}\penalty\ ,\qquad&i=n\penalty\ .\end{cases}

(3.5)

Let us denote by
\sigma_{i}
the Chern root of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. Importantly, the line bundles
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
generate the K-theory of the Fl
(n)
; indeed, they give us a so-called Toda presentation of the quantum K-theory
ahkmox
.

They are
{n(n-1)/2}
variables
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}
given to us by the quiver gauge theory, while the K-theory or cohomology of the complete flag can be expressed in terms of the smaller set of
n
variables
\sigma_{i}
.
At the level of classical cohomology, the Chern roots of the two classes of universal bundles are related to each other via the exact sequence:

0\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{S}_{i}\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{S}_{i+1}\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}\,\longrightarrow\,0\penalty\ .

(3.6)

This implies that the total Chern classes of the involved bundles are related as:

c(\mathcal{S}_{i})\,\wedge\,c(\mathcal{Q}_{i+1})\,=\,c(\mathcal{S}_{i+1})\penalty\ .

(3.7)

Expanding both sides, one obtains:

e_{b}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i+1)})\,=\,e_{b}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)})\,+\,\sigma_{i+1}\,e_{b-1}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)})\penalty\ ,\qquad b=1,\cdots,i+1\penalty\ ,

(3.8)

with the convention that
e_{0}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)})=1
for all
i
and
e_{b}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)})=0
whenever
b>i
and
b<0
.
In particular,

\sigma_{1}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(1)}\penalty\ .

(3.9)

For example, for Fl
(3)
, we have (
3.9
) together with:

\begin{split}&e_{1}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)})\,=\,e_{1}^{2}(\sigma)\penalty\ ,\qquad e_{2}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)})\,=\,e_{2}^{2}(\sigma)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.10)

Here, we are using the notation,
e_{i}^{j}(\sigma)\equiv e_{i}(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{j})
. For Fl
(3)
, we have three variables
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}
and three variables
\sigma_{i}
.

In the quantum cohomology, the classical relations (
3.7
) get deformed as follows
Gu:2023tcv
;
Gu:2023fpw
:

c(\mathcal{S}_{i})\,\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\,c(\mathcal{Q}_{i+1})\,=\,c(\mathcal{S}_{i+1})\,-\,\mathsf{q}_{i}\,c(\mathcal{S}_{i-1})\penalty\ .

(3.11)

More explicitly, one finds:

e_{b}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i+1)})\,=\,e_{b}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)})\,+\,\sigma_{i+1}\,e_{b-1}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)})\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{i}\,e_{b-2}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i-1)})\penalty\ ,\qquad b=1,\cdots,i+1\penalty\ .

(3.12)

From the 2d GLSM perspective, the quantum parameters
\mathsf{q}_{i}
are the exponentiated complexified FI parameters. Observe that the relation (
3.9
) is still valid at the quantum level; meanwhile, the higher-order relations are more involved. For example, for
{\rm QH}^{\bullet}
(Fl
(3)
) the classical relations (
3.10
) become:

\begin{split}&e_{1}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)})\,=\,e_{1}^{2}(\sigma)\penalty\ ,\qquad e_{2}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)})\,=\,e_{2}^{2}(\sigma)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.13)

Another example: relations in
{\rm QH}^{\bullet}({\rm Fl}(4))
.
Taking
n=4
, we find that, in addition to the relations (
3.9
) and (
3.13
), the quantum relations for Fl
(4)
include:

\begin{split}&e_{1}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(3)})\,=\,e_{1}^{3}(\sigma)\penalty\ ,\\
&e_{2}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(3)})\,=\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,+\,e_{2}^{3}(\sigma)\penalty\ ,\\
&e_{3}(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(3)})\,=\,\,e_{3}^{3}(\sigma)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,\sigma_{3}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.14)

This expresses the
6
variables
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}
in terms of the
4
variables
\sigma_{i}
.

3.2
3d GLSMs and quantum K-theory of complete flag manifolds

Consider the 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
uplift of the 2d GLSM considered in the previous subsection, where we take the 3d spacetime to be
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{1}_{\beta}
. We will fix the gauge Chern--Simons (CS) levels to be such that:
4
4

4

Here we are using the conventions reviewed in subsection 2.2 of
Closset:2023vos
for the CS levels.

\mathsf{k}_{i}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\qquad\mathsf{l}_{i}\,=\,-1\penalty\ ,

(3.15)

where
\mathsf{k}_{i}
and
\mathsf{l}_{i}
are the Chern–Simons levels of the 3d gauge group
U(i)_{\mathsf{k}_{i},\mathsf{k}_{i}+i\,\mathsf{l}_{i}}
for
i=1,\cdots,n-1
. Moreover, there is a mixed CS level
\mathsf{k}_{i,i{+}1}=\frac{1}{2}
between every two consecutive gauge groups
U(i)
and
U(i+1)
.

With this choice of CS levels, the 3d twisted chiral ring is known to be isomorphic to the quantum K-theory ring of the complete flag manifold. One way to see this is by looking at the Bethe ansatz equations (BAEs) coming from the 3d A-model subsector of the full theory. Motivated by BAE computations, the quantum K-theory relations can be compactly written in the following form
Gu:2023tcv
;
Gu:2023fpw
:

\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{S}_{i})\,\otimes_{q}\,\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{Q}_{i+1})=\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{S}_{i+1})\,-\,t\,\frac{q_{i}}{1-q_{i}}\,\det(\mathcal{Q}_{i+1})\,\otimes_{q}\,(\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{S}_{i})-\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{S}_{i-1}))\penalty\ ,

(3.16)

for
i=1,\cdots,n-1
, where
\otimes_{q}
is the product operation of the quantum K-theory ring. Here, for any vector bundle
E
,

\lambda_{t}(E)\equiv 1\,+\,t\,[E]\,+\,\cdots\,+\,t^{\text{rk}(E)}\,[\wedge^{\text{rk}(E)}E]\penalty\ ,

(3.17)

with
t
a formal parameter.
Taking
q_{i}=0
in the above equations, one obtains the classical K-theory ring relations of the complete flag manifold.
This presentation can also be related to the Toda presentation mentioned earlier, see

(
ahkmox
, theorems 5.7, 5.11)
.

In the field-theory language, the K-theoretic Chern roots of the tautological bundle
\mathcal{S}_{i}
are given by
x^{(i)}_{a_{i}}\sim e^{-\,\beta\,\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{a_{i}}}
where
\widetilde{\sigma}^{(i)}
is the 3d real adjoint scalar in the
U(i)
3d
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplet. Moreover, the quantum parameters
q_{i}\sim e^{-\,\beta\,\zeta_{i}}
are defined in terms of the 3d real FI parameters
\zeta_{i}
. With this in mind, and in terms of the corresponding Chern characters, the quantum relations (
3.16
) can be written more explicitly as:

e_{a}(x^{(i+1)})\,=\,e_{a}(x^{(i)})\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{i}}\,x_{i+1}\,\cdot\,e_{a-1}(x^{(i)})\,-\,\frac{q_{i}}{1\,-\,q_{i}}\,x_{i+1}\,\cdot\,e_{a-1}(x^{(i-1)})\penalty\ ,

(3.18)

for
a=1,\cdots,i+1
. Here
x_{i}\sim e^{-\,\beta\,\sigma_{i}}
is the K-theoretic Chern root of the quotient line bundle
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. In the above equation (
3.18
), we have adopted the convention that
q_{0}=0
,
e_{0}(x^{(i)})=1
and
e_{a}(x^{(i)})=0
when
a>i
or
a<0
. In particular, looking at
i=0
, the above relations become:

x_{1}\,=\,x_{1}^{(1)}\penalty\ .

(3.19)

Example I: QK relations for Fl
(3)
.
For the case
n=3
, in addition to (
3.19
), we find the following relations for the quantum K-theory ring of Fl
(3)
:

e_{1}(x^{(2)})\,=\,e_{1}^{2}(x)\penalty\ ,\qquad e_{2}(x^{(2)})\,=\,\frac{1}{1-q_{1}}\,e_{2}^{2}(x)\penalty\ ,

(3.20)

where,
e_{i}^{j}(x)\equiv e_{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{j})
are as defined in the previous subsection.

Example II: QK relations for Fl
(4)
.
In addition to (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), we find the following relations for the quantum K-theory of Fl
(4)
:

\begin{split}&e_{1}(x^{(3)})\,=\,e_{1}^{3}(x)\penalty\ ,\\
&e_{2}(x^{(3)})\,=\,\frac{1}{1-q_{1}}\,x_{1}\,x_{2}\,+\,\frac{1}{1-q_{2}}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}\,+\,x_{1}\,x_{3}\penalty\ ,\\
&e_{3}(x^{(3)})\,=\,\frac{1}{1-q_{1}}\,\frac{1}{1-q_{2}}\,e_{3}^{3}(x)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(3.21)

2d limit: back to quantum cohomology.
In the limit of small circle radius
\beta
, the quantum K-theory relations (
3.16
) reduce to the quantum cohomology relations (
3.11
), keeping in mind that the quantum parameters are related by:

q_{i}\,=\,\beta^{2}\,\mathsf{q}_{i}\penalty\ ,\qquad i=1,\cdots,n-1\penalty\ .

(3.22)

For example, the relations (
3.20
) reduce to (
3.13
), and (
3.21
) reduce to (
3.14
) in this limit.

4
Schubert line defects for complete flag manifolds

In this section, we first review the definition of the Schubert varieties in the complete flag manifold. We then construct and study the corresponding Schubert line defects in the 3d GLSM.

4.1
Schubert varieties in complete flag manifolds

The Schubert varieties of the complete flag manifold Fl
(n)
(
3.1
) are indexed by the elements of the symmetric group
S_{n}
— that is, by permutations. There are thus
n!
distinct Schubert varieties in Fl
(n)
, matching the topological Euler characteristic (
3.4
). Note that Schubert varieties can be defined in terms of fixed points of the
SL(n)
action on Fl
(n)
—- we refer to
Anderson_Fulton_2023
for a detailed account.

For a given permutation
w\in S_{n}
, we define the corresponding Schubert variety
X_{w}
as follows
(
Anderson_Fulton_2023
, page 153)
:

X_{w}\,:=\,\left\{\,F_{\bullet}\in{\rm Fl}(n)\,|\,\dim(F_{i}\cap E_{j})\geq{\rm r}^{w_{0}w}_{i,j}\penalty\ ,\quad\forall\,1\leq i,j\leq n\,\right\}\penalty\ .

(4.1)

Here,
w_{0}=(n\,\cdots\,2\,1)
is the longest permutation in
S_{n}
, and:

(w_{0}w)(i)\,=\,n\,+\,1\,-\,w(i)\penalty\ .

(4.2)

Moreover,
E_{\bullet}
in (
4.1
) denotes the reference flag
(
Anderson_Fulton_2023
, page 171)
:

E_{\bullet}\>:=\>\left(E_{1}\,\subset\,E_{2}\,\subset\,\cdots\,\subset\,E_{n}\,\equiv\,{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\right)\penalty\ ,

(4.3)

with
E_{i}\;:=\;{\rm Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{{\rm e}_{n+1-i},\cdots,{\rm e}_{n}\}
. Here,
\{{\rm e}_{1},\cdots,{\rm e}_{n}\}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Finally, the rank matrix
{\rm r}^{w}
associated with a permutation
w
is defined as:

{\rm r}^{w}_{i,j}\,:=\,\#\{\,l\,\leq\,i\,:\,w(l)\,\leq\,j\,\}\penalty\ .

(4.4)

The Schubert variety
X_{w}
is the closure of the Schubert cell
X_{w}^{\circ}
, which is defined as in (
4.1
) with equalities instead of inequalities. Note that the Schubert variety associated with the identity permutation
(\,1\,2\,\cdots n\,)
is
X={\rm Fl}(n)
itself, while the Schubert variety associated with the longest permutation
w_{0}
is the point class. More generally, the codimension of a Schubert variety
X_{w}
is given by the length of
w
:

{\rm codim}(X_{w})\,=\,\ell(w)\penalty\ .

(4.5)

See (
A.1
) for the definition of
\ell(w)
. Inclusion relations of Schubert varieties follow the Bruhat order on
S_{n}
; in particular,
X_{w}\subset X_{w^{\prime}}
implies that
\ell(w)>\ell(w^{\prime})
.

Schubert classes.
The cohomological or K-theoretic Schubert classes associated with the Schubert varieties form a basis for the cohomology or K-theory of Fl
(n)
. We refer to appendix
A
for more details on the known presentations of the Schubert classes as polynomials. Briefly, in cohomology, the Schubert classes
[X_{w}]
associated with the Schubert varieties form a basis of the cohomology ring. In (equivariant) classical cohomology H
{}^{\bullet}_{T}({\rm Fl}(n))
, these are represented by the (double) Schubert polynomials
\mathfrak{S}_{w}
(
A.8
), and by quantum (double) Schubert polynomials
\mathfrak{S}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}
(
A.18
) in the (equivariant) quantum cohomology ring QH
{}_{T}^{\bullet}({\rm Fl}(n))
. See appendix
A.1
for more details and explicit examples.

Meanwhile, in the K-theory, the Schubert classes
[\mathcal{O}_{w}]
are the equivalence classes of the structure sheaves on the Schubert varieties. In classical (equivariant) K-theory K
{}_{T}({\rm Fl}(n))
, these are represented by the (double) Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}_{w}
(
A.26
), which are upgraded to quantum (double) Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{w}
(
A.32
) in the (equivariant) quantum K-theory QK
{}_{T}({\rm Fl}(n))
. See appendix
A.2
for more details and examples.

4.2
Schubert line defects in the 3d GLSM

Let us now describe a systematic construction of line defects in the 3d GLSM of the complete flag manifold that flow to the Schubert classes in the quantum K-theory. We call these defects the Schubert line defects. By a reasoning similar to the one in
Closset:2023bdr
;
Gu:2025tda
, we will show that the insertion of these line defects restricts the target geometry of the 3d GLSM at the point of insertion to the corresponding Schubert varieties.

The Schubert line defects are realized by coupling a
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM quiver to the 3d GLSM described in section
3
. In the following, we will first directly present the proposal, and then study its vacuum equations to show that the target geometry is indeed restricted to the Schubert varieties. We will actually present a stronger result, namely that the 1d-3d system provides a resolution of the (generally singular) Schubert variety. Furthermore, we will study the flavored Witten index of the 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
quiver. In all the examples we have checked, the index precisely agrees with the corresponding quantum Grothendieck polynomial once we use the quantum K-theory relations (
3.18
) to express them as polynomials in the
x_{i}
variables.

4.2.1
General proposal

Figure 3
:
Quiver diagram of the 1d-3d coupled system realizing
X_{w}\subseteq{\rm Fl}(n)
. The last column represents the 3d GLSM to the complete flag manifold Fl
(n)
. The square quiver in the blue box is the 1d quiver gauge theory defining the defect. The circle nodes represent 1d gauge groups of the indicated ranks — for compactness, we write
{\rm r}_{i,j}\equiv{\rm r}_{i,j}^{w_{0}w}
, and
{\rm r}_{i}\equiv{\rm r}_{i,i}
. Horizontal and vertical arrows stand for 1d chiral matter multiplets in the bifundamental representation of the corresponding nodes. Moreover, diagonal arrows correspond to 1d Fermi multiplets in the bifundamental representation of the corresponding nodes. The gray squares in the last row represent fixed background data for the inclusion maps defined in (
4.8
).

For the Schubert variety
X_{w}
defined in equation (
4.1
), we construct the corresponding Schubert defects by coupling a 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM to the 3d GLSM described in section
3
. The resulting 1d-3d coupled theory can be realized as the quiver diagram displayed in figure
2
. The last column alone gives the 3d GLSM for the complete flag manifold, and we use a blue box to encircle the 1d theory of the Schubert defect.

In the quiver, circle nodes denote unitary gauge groups of the indicated ranks, while the square nodes denote unitary flavor groups. Solid arrows represent chiral multiplets, and dashed arrows correspond to Fermi multiplets. Both types of multiplets live in the bifundamental representations of the corresponding groups. We adopt the convention that each multiplet lives in the fundamental representation under the group of the source node, and the anti-fundamental representation under the group of the target node.

More specifically, the circle node at position
(i,j)
(that is, the
i
-th row and the
j
-th column) for
i,j=1,2,\cdots,n-1
, corresponds to a 1d gauge group factor
U({\rm r}_{i,j})
, where
\text{r}_{i,j}
is a short hand notation for
\text{r}_{i,j}^{w_{0}w}
defined as in (
4.4
), namely:

\text{r}_{i,j}\equiv\#\{l\leq i\mid n+1-w(l)\leq j\}\penalty\ .

(4.6)

We use the following notational conventions for the 1d-3d quiver theory. Bulk 3d chiral multiplets are denoted by
\phi
, while defect chiral multiplets are denoted by
\varphi
, and defect Fermi multiplets by
\Gamma
. For bulk chiral multiplets, we use the notation
\phi_{\ell}^{\ell+1}
to indicate the bifundamental between the gauge groups
U(\ell)
and
U(\ell+1)
in the last column of the quiver. For defect fields, superscripts and subscripts are used to indicate the direction of the corresponding arrow: a field
\varphi_{i,j}^{k,l}
transforms in the fundamental representation of the unitary group at the source node
(i,j)
and in the anti-fundamental of the group at the target node
(k,l)
, and similarly for the Fermi multiplets
\Gamma_{i,j}^{k,l}
.

Therefore, for
i,j=1,2,\cdots,n-1
, we have chiral multiplets
\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}
corresponding to horizontal arrows, and
\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}
corresponding to vertical arrows. We also have Fermi multiplets
\Gamma_{i,j}^{i+1,j+1}
corresponding to the diagonal arrows in each block.

The last row in the quiver of figure
3
needs special treatment. Instead of the product structure
U(1)\times U(2)\times\cdots\times U(n)
that is common for quiver gauge theories, what we really mean here is that we chose a flavor symmetry group structure

U(1)\subset U(2)\subset\cdots\subset U(n)\penalty\ ,

(4.7)

which corresponds to a choice of reference flag
E_{\bullet}
.
Hence, the horizontal arrows in the last row are not dynamical fields, but represent fixed background data. For the arrow between flavor
U(i)
and
U(i+1)
, we will denote the background field by
\iota^{(i)}
, and it has the following form as an
i\times(i+1)
matrix:

\iota^{(i)}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}_{i\times i}&0_{i\times 1}\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ .

(4.8)

In the quiver diagram, we indicate these background arrows in gray.

To describe the interaction terms, we need to specify the
E
-term and
J
-term for each Fermi multiplet
\Gamma_{i,j}^{i+1,j+1}
. In our construction, the
J
-terms are set to be zero, while the
E
-terms are given as follows:
5
5

5

This can also be formally encoded in a gauge-invariant superpotential (see
(
Closset:2025zyl
, equation (2.12))
)

W=\text{Tr}\,\left(\,\overline{\Gamma}_{i,j}^{i+1,j+1}\,(\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1}\,-\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i+1,j}^{i+1,j+1})\,\right)\penalty\ .

(4.9)

It should be noted, however, that this expression is solely formal. It is used in the mathematical description of graded quivers with potential to encode relations in a convenient manner — see
e.g.

Franco:2017lpa
;
Closset:2018axq
.

\overline{D}_{+}\,\Gamma_{i,j}^{i+1,j+1}\,=\,E_{i,j}^{i+1,j+1}\,=\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1}\,-\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i+1,j}^{i+1,j+1}\penalty\ ,

(4.10)

where
\cdot
denotes matrix multiplication, and
\varphi_{n,j}^{n,j+1}\equiv\iota^{(j)}
,
\varphi_{i,n}^{i+1,n}\equiv\phi_{i}^{i+1}
are understood.

4.2.2
Vacuum equations

To uncover the underlying geometry, let us first study the vacuum equations. We choose a phase of the
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM where all the 1d FI parameters are taken to be positive. Let us denote the 1d FI parameter associated with
U(1)\subseteq U({\rm r}_{i,j})
by
\xi_{i,j}
.

D
-term equations.

The
D
-term equation for the gauge group
U(\text{r}_{i,j})
is given by:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\cdot(\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j})^{\dagger}+\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\cdot(\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1})^{\dagger}-(\varphi_{i-1,j}^{i,j})^{\dagger}\cdot\varphi_{i-1,j}^{i,j}-(\varphi_{i,j-1}^{i,j})^{\dagger}\cdot\varphi_{i,j-1}^{i,j}=\xi_{i,j}\,\mathbb{I}_{\text{r}_{i,j}}\penalty\ .

(4.11)

Note that, for certain edge cases, some of these fields may be absent. The positive terms on the LHS correspond to chiral multiplets transforming in the fundamental representation of the gauge group, while the negative terms correspond to those transforming in the anti-fundamental representation.

Each term of the schematic form
AA^{\dagger}
or
A^{\dagger}A
is a Hermitian matrix that is positive semi-definite, while
\xi_{i,j}\mathbb{I}_{\text{r}_{i,j}}
is positive definite for
\xi_{i,j}>0
. Hence, we can conclude that:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,(\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j})^{\dagger}\,+\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,(\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1})^{\dagger}\,\in\,\mathbb{H}^{+}_{\,\text{r}_{i,j}}\penalty\ .

(4.12)

Here, we denote the set of positive definite Hermitian matrices of size
\text{r}_{i,j}\times\text{r}_{i,j}
by
\mathbb{H}^{+}_{\,\text{r}_{i,j}}
.
If one of
\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}
and
\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}
is absent, we can conclude that the remaining one corresponds to a matrix of full rank. But, when both of them are present (which is our current case for defect gauge groups), we cannot conclude that both of them correspond to full rank matrices. We have to resort to the
E
-term equations.

E
-term equations.

The supersymmetric ground state conditions require the vanishing of the
E
-terms.
For the
E
term given in equation (
4.10
), we then have

\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1}\,-\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i+1,j}^{i+1,j+1}\,=\,0\penalty\ .

(4.13)

Rank constraints from the vacuum equations.

Combining the
D
-term equations (
4.12
) with the
E
-term equations (
4.13
), we see that, if
\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1}
is a matrix of full rank, then so is
\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}
; similarly if
\varphi_{i+1,j}^{i+1,j+1}
is a matrix of full rank, then so is
\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}
. To make the notation more intuitive, one can think in terms of a square in the quiver: combining the
D
-term equation for the top-left gauge group, and the
E
-term equation for the diagonal Fermi, we see that, if the bottom arrow corresponds to a full rank matrix, so does the top arrow; if the right arrow corresponds to a full rank matrix, so does the left arrow.

In what follows, we prove the first statement, and the second statement can be verified using an analogous argument. Indeed, suppose
\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1}
corresponds to a matrix of full rank, then we have:

A\,\equiv\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1}\,\cdot\,(\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1})^{\dagger}\,\in\,\mathbb{H}_{\,\text{r}_{i,j+1}}^{+}\penalty\ ,

(4.14)

and
A
is invertible. Multiplying both sides of the
E
-term equation (
4.13
) on the right by
(\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1})^{\dagger}\cdot A^{-1}
, we can solve for
\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}
to obtain:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,=\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i+1,j}^{i+1,j+1}\,\cdot\,(\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i+1,j+1})^{\dagger}\,\cdot\,A^{-1}\,\equiv\,\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,B\penalty\ .

(4.15)

Plugging this back into equation (
4.12
), we have:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\,(\,\mathbb{I}_{\text{r}_{i+1,j}}\,+\,B\,\cdot\,B^{\dagger}\,)\,\cdot\,(\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j})^{\dagger}\,\in\,\mathbb{H}^{+}_{\,\text{r}_{i,j}}\penalty\ .

(4.16)

Hence
\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}
is an
\text{r}_{i,j}\times\text{r}_{i+1,j}
matrix of full rank. Otherwise there would be a non-zero vector
v
of size
1\times\text{r}_{i,j}
, such that
v\cdot\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}=0
, contradicting equation (
4.16
). This concludes our proof.

With this result in hand, we can show by induction that all the chiral multiplets in the quiver correspond to full rank matrices.
For our quiver in figure
3
, the gray arrows in the last row correspond to full rank matrices by construction, as given in equation (
4.8
), while the arrows in the last column correspond to the bulk fields and we can use the bulk
D
-term equations to show that they all correspond to full rank matrices (since each bulk gauge group only has one set of chiral multiplets in the fundamental representation). These provide the base cases for the induction.
Applying the argument above to each square of the quiver then propagates the full rank condition inward, and we conclude that all chiral multiplets in the quiver correspond to full rank matrices.

4.2.3
The underlying geometry

To understand why the bulk geometry gets restricted to the corresponding Schubert varieties, there are two complementary perspectives. First, one can analyze the geometry of the full 1d-3d coupled system and then project onto the bulk direction. Second, one can instead focus directly on the bulk fields and study how the defect restricts the bulk fields through the
E
-term relations. We now describe both viewpoints.

Geometry of the coupled 1d-3d system.
We have already shown that all the chiral multiplets correspond to full rank matrices. Thus, they can be interpreted as inclusions of vector spaces. For example, the chiral field
\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}
can be viewed as an
\text{r}_{i,j}\times\text{r}_{i,j+1}
matrix of full rank, describing the inclusion of an
\text{r}_{i,j}
-dimensional vector space into an
\text{r}_{i,j+1}
-dimensional vector space, with the gauge symmetries acting by rotations of the basis in the corresponding vector spaces. The
E
-term equations can be understood as commutativity relations, which ensure that these inclusions are compatible: the composition of maps along different paths connecting the same nodes gives the same subspace in the target vector space. Finally, the chiral multiplets in the last row are not dynamical, and they describe a fixed complete flag
E_{1}\subset E_{2}\subset\cdots\subset E_{n}
. Therefore, the full collection of chiral multiplets and their relations describes all possible consistent configurations of nested subspaces of the form shown in figure
4
, where
\dim(V_{i,j})=\text{r}_{i,j}
and
\dim(F_{i})=i
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{1,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{1,n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{2,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{2,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{2,n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{n-1,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{n-1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{n-1,n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}E_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}E_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}

Figure 4
:
Diagram of nested vector spaces in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, with a fixed reference flag
E_{\bullet}
shown in gray. The arrows indicate the inclusions of subspaces, while the circular arrows indicate that the squares commute. Here,
\dim(V_{i,j})=\text{r}_{i,j}
and
\dim(F_{i})=i
.

In passing, this setup can be seen as a generalization of Grassmannian
\text{Gr}(k,n)
, which describes all possible
k
-dimensional subspaces of an
n
-dimensional vector space. Mathematically, this geometry of our 1d-3d system corresponds to a particular quiver Grassmannian
iezzi2025quivergrassmanniansbottsamelsonresolution
or, equivalently, to bioriented flags
cibotaru2020bioriented
. Both of these constructions have been proven to be isomorphic to so-called Bott–Samelson resolutions
BottSamelsonResolution
of Schubert varieties in Fl
(n)
(for a particular presentation of each permutation) — we discuss these mathematical constructions in more detail in appendix
B
.
The 1d-3d target geometry
\widetilde{X}_{w}
therefore serves as a resolution of the singular Schubert variety
X_{w}
. The projection map

\pi\,:\,\widetilde{X}_{w}\,\to\,X_{w}\penalty\ ,

(4.17)

sends each configuration in figure
4
to its embedding into the last column, which corresponds to the projection onto the Schubert variety
X_{w}\subseteq X
inside the bulk geometry.

Indeed, projecting to the bulk geometry, one can see that the complete flag
F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset\cdots\subset F_{n-1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
satisfies the incidence relations defining Schubert varieties as in equation (
4.1
):

\dim(F_{i}\,\cap\,E_{j})\,\geq\,\dim(V_{i,j}\,\cap\,E_{j})\,=\,\dim(V_{i,j})\,=\,\text{r}_{i,j}\penalty\ ,

(4.18)

where in the first step, we used the fact that
V_{i,j}\subset F_{i}
, and in the second step, we used the fact that
V_{i,j}\subset E_{j}
, as shown in figure
4
.
For this check,
i
and
j
range from
1
to
n-1
. Note that the condition
\dim(F_{i}\cap E_{j})\geq{\rm r}_{i,j}
is automatically satisfied whenever
i=n
or
j=n
.
Therefore, the bulk geometry is given by the corresponding Schubert variety
X_{w}
.

Bulk geometry via
E
-term constraints.

One can also study the bulk geometry directly by considering all the constraints on the bulk fields
\phi_{\ell}^{\ell+1}
arising from the
E
-term equations. Individually, the
E
-term equations (
4.13
) do not impose constraints on the bulk fields. We have to combine them so that both the fixed background
\iota^{(i)}
and the bulk fields
\phi_{\ell}^{\ell+1}
are involved simultaneously.

To that end, consider two paths in the quiver connecting the
U(\text{r}_{i,j})
node and the flavor
U(n)
node. The first path runs horizontally and then vertically, leading to a composed field:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i,j+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,n-1}^{i,n}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i}^{i+1}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i+1}^{i+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\phi_{n-1}^{n}\penalty\ .

(4.19)

The second path runs vertically and then horizontally, leading to another composed field:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i+1,j}\,\cdot\varphi_{i+1,j}^{i+2,j}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\varphi_{n-1,j}^{n,j}\,\cdot\,\iota^{(j)}\,\cdot\,\iota^{(j+1)}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\iota^{(n-1)}\penalty\ ,

(4.20)

where the background fields
\iota
’s are given in equation (
4.8
). Commutativity of the quiver implies that these two compositions must coincide; equivalently, one can obtain their equality by repeatedly applying the
E
-term equations.

Now, due to the special form of the fixed background
\iota
’s, we have:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i,j+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,n-1}^{i,n}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i}^{i+1}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i+1}^{i+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\phi_{n-1}^{n}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}*_{j\times j}&0_{j\times(n-j)}\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ ,

(4.21)

where the block form on the RHS arises from plugging
\iota
’s into the composed field (
4.20
).

The composed field
\phi_{i}^{i+1}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i+1}^{i+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\phi_{n-1}^{n}
is an
i\times n
full rank matrix, describing
F_{i}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and we consider the submatrix
A_{i\times(n-j)}
formed by its last
(n-j)
columns, which is not necessarily of full rank. Then the above equation implies that:

\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i,j+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,n-1}^{i,n}\,\cdot\,A_{i\times(n-j)}\,=\,0\penalty\ .

(4.22)

The composed field
\varphi_{i,j}^{i,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,j+1}^{i,j+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\varphi_{i,n-1}^{i,n}
is an
\text{r}_{i,j}\times i
full-rank matrix, and consequently,

\text{rk}(A_{i\times(n-j)})\,\leq\,i-\text{r}_{i,j}\penalty\ .

(4.23)

This condition is actually equivalent to the defining incidence relation
\dim(F_{i}\cap E_{j})\geq\text{r}_{i,j}
. The vector space
F_{i}
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is represented by an
i\times n
full rank matrix:

\phi_{i}^{i+1}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i+1}^{i+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\phi_{n-1}^{n}
,

whose rows can be viewed as
n
-vectors, spanning
F_{i}
.
Then any
n
-vector
v_{1\times n}\in F_{i}
can be written as a linear combination of these basis vectors:

v_{1\times n}\,=\,w_{1\times i}\,\cdot\,\left(\,\phi_{i}^{i+1}\,\cdot\,\phi_{i+1}^{i+2}\,\cdot\,\cdots\,\cdot\,\phi_{n-1}^{n}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

(4.24)

where
w
is an
i
-vector of the coefficients.
If we further want this
n
-vector
v
to lie in
F_{i}\cap E_{j}
, its last
n-j
entries need to vanish:

w_{1\times i}\,\cdot\,A_{i\times(n-j)}\,=\,0\penalty\ .

(4.25)

That is,
w_{1\times i}
has to lie in the kernel of
A_{i\times(n-j)}
. The set of all
n
-vectors
v
in
F_{i}\cap E_{j}
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of
i
-vectors
w
satisfying
w\cdot A=0
. Therefore, we have:

\dim(F_{i}\,\cap\,E_{j})\,=\,\dim(\ker(A))\,=\,i\,-\,\text{rk}(A)\,\geq\,\text{r}_{i,j}\penalty\ ,

(4.26)

where in the second step we used the rank-nullity theorem, and in the last step we used the rank constraint (
4.23
).
Again, we see that the bulk geometry gets restricted to the Schubert variety.

Further comment.
Although both perspectives show that the bulk geometry is restricted to the corresponding Schubert variety
X_{w}
, the first approach also reveals something stronger: the underlying geometry of the full 1d-3d system is a resolution (
4.17
) of
X_{w}
.
Thus, the coupled system contains additional geometric structure beyond simply requiring the bulk fields to lie in
X_{w}
. The additional 1d degrees of freedom on the line defect provide us with precisely the ingredients needed to engineer the smooth resolution
\widetilde{X}_{w}
as a target space.

As we will show in subsection
4.3
, the flavored Witten index of the 1d gauge theory produces the Chern character ch
(\mathcal{O}_{X_{w}})
of the structure sheaf on
X_{w}
. This is consistent with the fact that the pushforward of the structure sheaf on
\widetilde{X}_{w}
reproduces the structure sheaf on
X_{w}

(
brion-lec
, Proposition 2.2.5)
,
(
brion-kumar
, section 3.4)
:

\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}_{w}}\,=\,\mathcal{O}_{X_{w}}\penalty\ ,

(4.27)

so that their K-theory classes, and their Chern characters, coincide. Note that what we previously called
\mathcal{O}_{w}
is a shorthand notation for
\mathcal{O}_{X_{w}}
here.

4.2.4
Mass terms induced by background fields

Due to the existence of the non-dynamical fields, certain chiral fields and Fermi fields are massive and can be integrated out to obtain the infrared physics.
To see this in general, consider a Fermi multiplet
\Gamma=(\eta,...)
whose
E
-term contains a term proportional to a single chiral multiplet
\Phi=(\phi,\psi)
, say
E(\Phi)\,=\,\mu\,\Phi
. Then the Lagrangian contains the following term:

\overline{\eta}\,\frac{\partial E(\phi)}{\partial\phi}\,\psi\,=\,\mu\,\overline{\eta}\,\psi\penalty\ ,

(4.28)

which generates a complex mass
\mu
for the pair
(\Gamma,\Phi)
. For a detailed discussion of 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry, see, for example, section 2 of
Closset:2025zyl
.

Consider the block in the quiver whose diagonal arrow is the Fermi multiplet
\Gamma_{n-1,j}^{n,j+1}
for
j=1,2,\cdots,n-1
. For simplicity, let us denote it by
\Gamma^{(j)}
. Its corresponding
E
-term interaction is given by:

\overline{D}_{+}\Gamma^{(j)}\>=\>\varphi_{n-1,j}^{n,j}\,\cdot\,\iota^{(j)}\,-\,\varphi_{n-1,j}^{n-1,j+1}\,\cdot\,\varphi_{n-1,j+1}^{n,j+1}\penalty\ .

(4.29)

Here,
\Gamma^{(j)}
is viewed as an
\text{r}_{n-1,j}\times(j+1)
matrix.
We denote the
(j+1)
columns of
\Gamma^{(j)}
by:

\Gamma_{1}^{(j)}\penalty\ ,\,\Gamma_{2}^{(j)}\penalty\ ,\,\cdots\,,\,\Gamma_{j+1}^{(j)}\penalty\ ,

(4.30)

each transforming in the fundamental representation of
U(\text{r}_{n-1,j})
.

The chiral multiplet
\varphi_{n-1,j}^{n,j}
can be viewed as an
\text{r}_{n-1,j}\times j
matrix, whose columns will be denoted by:

\varphi^{(j)}_{1}\,,\,\varphi^{(j)}_{2}\,,\,\cdots\,,\varphi^{(j)}_{j}\penalty\ ,

(4.31)

each transforming in the fundamental representation of
U(\text{r}_{n-1,j})
.

Since the background field
\iota^{(j)}
takes a special form
\iota^{(j)}=(\mathbb{I}_{j\times j}\quad 0_{j\times 1})
, the first term in (
4.29
)
reduces to
mass terms for the pairs
(\Gamma^{(j)}_{i},\varphi^{(j)}_{i})
for
i
from
1
to
j
. Then, in the infrared, integrating out these massive fields, the vertical chiral multiplets are absent, and we are left with only one Fermi multiplet
\Gamma^{(j)}_{j+1}
in the fundamental representation of
U(\text{r}_{n-1,j})
.

Therefore, the last row in figure
3
can be reduced, and we obtain the quiver shown in figure
5
. The Fermi multiplets in the last row are no longer bifundamentals, but instead reduce to single components
\Gamma^{(j)}_{j+1}
, each coupled to a distinct
U(1)\subset U(1)^{n}\subset U(n)
. We use the color red to make this distinction that they are only charged under one
U(1)
factor in the flavor group associated with the target node. In order to keep track of these charges, we assign the flavor fugacities as discussed next.

Figure 5
:
The 1d-3d coupled system after integrating out the massive 1d fermionic and bosonic degrees of freedom from the defect given in figure
3
. In the last row, the red diagonal arrows now represent Fermi multiplets in the fundamental representation of the gauge group represented by the circle node and have a charge
-1
under the
U(1)
subgroup of the bulk flavor symmetry
SU(n)
indexed by the square node.

Flavor fugacity assignment: a convention.

In the computation of the flavored Witten index of the 1d theory (discussed in the next subsection), one can turn on flavor fugacities associated with the flavor symmetry groups — this corresponds to probing the equivariant quantum K-theory of the complete flag manifold. Due to the inclusion maps, and as part of our conventions, the flavor fugacities associated with
U(j)
in the last row of the quiver are
y_{n},\cdots,y_{n-j+1}
. The surviving Fermi multiplet
\Gamma^{(j)}_{j+1}
charged under
U(1)\subset U(1)^{j+1}\subset U(j+1)
corresponds to the fugacity
y_{n-j}
. The pair
(\Gamma^{(j)}_{i},\varphi^{(j)}_{i})
carries fugacities
y_{n+1-i}
, and their contributions cancel out in the flavored Witten index, providing a consistency check that integrating out these fields is valid and does not affect the counting of supersymmetric ground states.

The bulk gauge groups are also treated as flavor symmetry groups from the 1d perspective. For each
U(i)
in the last column of the quiver, we denote the flavor fugacities by
x^{(i)}_{1},x^{(i)}_{2},\cdots x^{(i)}_{i}
for
i
from
1
to
n-1
, which, as we discussed in subsection
3.2
, are the K-theoretic Chern roots of the tautological bundle
\mathcal{S}_{i}
on the flag manifold.

4.3
1d indices as Chern characters of the Schubert classes

In the above discussion, we argued that, for a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, the proposed 1d line defect
{\mathscr{L}}_{w}
restricts the bulk scalar fields
\phi_{i}^{i+1}
at the point of insertion to land onto the Schubert variety
X_{w}
inside the complete flag. On general ground, the Witten index of the defining quiver gauge theory should give us the Chern character of the Schubert class
[\mathcal{O}_{w}]
in the equivariant quantum K-theory of the flag manifold. These characters are given in terms of the quantum double Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(q)}(x,y)
. See appendix
A.2
for further details. Checking this relationship would constitute a very strong check of our proposal.

Following the supersymmetric localization results of
Hori:2014tda
, and using the conventions spelled out in
Closset:2023bdr
;
Closset:2025zyl
, the 1d index of the quiver in figure
5
can be written as a so-called Jeffrey–Kirwan (JK) residue:

\mathcal{I}^{({\rm 1d})}_{w}(x,y)=\oint_{\rm JK}\;({\rm dM})\;{\rm Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm ver}\;{\rm Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm hor}\;{\rm Z}_{\rm Fermi}^{\rm black}\;{\rm Z}_{\rm Fermi}^{\rm red}\penalty\ .

(4.32)

The measure
({\rm dM})
is given by:

({\rm dM})\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,\Delta^{(i,j)}(z)\,\frac{1}{{\rm r}_{i,j}\,!}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\frac{-\,dz_{\alpha}^{(i,j)}}{2\pi i\,z_{\alpha}^{(i,j)}}\,\right]\penalty\ ,

(4.33)

with the 1d ranks defined in (
4.6
), and
\Delta^{(i,j)}
the Vandermonde determinant factor coming from the 1d
U({\rm r}_{i,j})
gauge W-bosons:

\Delta^{(i,j)}(z)\,:=\,\prod_{1\leq\alpha\neq\beta\leq{\rm r}_{i,j}}\left(1-\frac{z^{(i,j)}_{\alpha}}{z^{(i,j)}_{\beta}}\right)\penalty\ .

(4.34)

As for the different components appearing in the integrand of (
4.32
), these are the 1-loop contributions of the chiral and Fermi multiplets. More explicitly:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm ver}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\prod_{\beta=1}^{{\rm r}_{i+1,j}}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z^{(i,j)}_{\alpha}}{z^{(i+1,j)}_{\beta}}\,\right)^{-1}\penalty\ ,\\
&{\rm Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm hor}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,n-1}}\,\prod_{a=1}^{i}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z_{\alpha}^{(i,n-1)}}{x_{a}^{(i)}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-2}\,\prod_{\beta=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\prod_{\gamma=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j+1}}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z^{(i,j)}_{\beta}}{z^{(i,j+1)}_{\gamma}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\right]\penalty\ ,\\
&{\rm Z}^{\rm black}_{\rm Fermi}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\,\left[\,\prod_{\gamma=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,n-1}}\prod_{a=1}^{i+1}\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z^{(i,n-1)}_{\gamma}}{x_{a}^{(i+1)}}\,\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n-2}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\prod_{\beta=1}^{{\rm r}_{i+1,j+1}}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z^{(i,j)}_{\alpha}}{z_{\beta}^{(i+1,j+1)}}\,\right)\,\right]\,\penalty\ ,\\
&{\rm Z}^{\rm red}_{\rm Fermi}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{n-1,i}}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z^{(n-1,i)}_{\alpha}}{y_{n-i}}\,\right)\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(4.35)

Let us make the following remarks:

•

The form of the final contribution in (
4.35
) is obtained after the cancellation that occurs between the massive chiral and Fermi multiplets in the last row in figure
3
, leading to figure
5
, as explained in subsection
4.2.4
.

•

Due to the fact that all 1d real FI parameters are taken to be positive, the JK prescription instructs us to pick only the poles coming from the chiral fields in the fundamental representation. That is, those in
{\rm Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm ver}
and
{\rm Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm hor}
, but not the poles coming from the measure
({\rm dM})
. One should take the JK residue iteratively, starting from the first row and working from left to right, then proceeding to the second row in the same way, and continuing row by row. (Equivalently, one can proceed column by column from left to right.)

•

When working out the residue integrals in (
4.32
), we end up with polynomial expressions in terms of the K-theoretic Chern roots
x^{(\bullet)}_{\bullet}
of the tautological vector bundles
\mathcal{S}_{\bullet}
. Since the Grothendieck polynomials defined in appendix
A.2
are given in terms of the roots of the quotient bundles
\mathcal{Q}_{\bullet}
, we further need to use the quantum relations (
3.18
).
6
6

6

In fact, if we instead use the classical ring relations obtained by taking
q_{i}=0
in (
3.16
), we end up with the classical Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}_{w}(x,y)
. These are the Chern characters of the Schubert classes in the classical K-theory ring of Fl
(n)
. See appendix
A.2
for the definition and further properties of these polynomials. The original index in terms of
x^{(\bullet)}_{\bullet}
is believed to coincide with the universal Grothendieck polynomial
buch2005grothendieck
.

More specifically, we can start from the index, which is symmetric in the K-theoretic Chern roots of
\mathcal{S}_{i}
(by symmetry, from the 1d perspective). Using the quantum K-theory ring relation (
3.18
), we may eliminate the elementary symmetric polynomials in
x^{(i+1)}
, in terms of those in
x^{(i)}
and
x^{(i-1)}
, and in terms of the quotient bundle Chern roots
x_{i+1}
.
Iterating this procedure, we eventually reach the base case in which
x^{(1)}_{1}
is identified with
x_{1}
, leaving an expression entirely in terms of the quotient bundle Chern roots, which should give us the expected quantum Grothendieck polynomial. (One can systematize this procedure on a computer using Gröbner bases methods, as in
Closset:2023vos
.)
Note that one cannot go the other way around, hence the Witten indices carry strictly more information than the quantum Grothendieck polynomials. (If we were to start from the latter, given in terms of the quotient-bundle Chern roots, one can eliminate
x_{i+1}
using equation (
3.18
). But this elimination is not unique as equation (
3.18
) contains a family of identities (labeled by the index
a
), and different choices of
a
lead to different ways of eliminating
x_{i+1}
.)

•

To speed up the calculation of the Witten indices, one can locally apply the 1d Seiberg-like dualities that we reviewed in section
2
. As mentioned earlier, this amounts to ‘confining’ some of the 1d gauge nodes that, from the geometric perspective discussed above, lead to redundant constraints.

4.4
Example: Schubert defects for Fl
(3)

As a pedagogical example, let us look at the case
n=3
, in which case we have six Schubert varieties indexed by
w\in S_{3}
. For each permutation, the corresponding line defect is shown in figure
6
. In figure
7
, we show the simplified 1d quivers obtained after using the 1d duality moves discussed in section
2.2
. Note that the arrows in the Hasse diagram denote the projections of the Schubert varieties (that is,
X_{w}\rightarrow X_{w^{\prime}}
if
X_{w^{\prime}}\subset X_{w}
).
Looking at these defects as 1d-3d coupled systems, one can compute the indices of the defect SQM using the JK residue formula (
4.32
).

Figure 6
:
Hasse diagram for the Schubert line defects of Fl
(3)

(compare
e.g.
to
(
Billey-Lakshmibai-2000
, example 4.8.6)
).
For each one of the 1d quiver defects, the associated
S_{3}
permutation is displayed next to it. The arrows connecting different defects correspond to a transposition connecting the indexing permutations. In our notation,
(1\,2\,3)
is the identity, corresponding to
X={\rm Fl}(3)
.

Figure 7
:
Hasse diagram for the defects in Fl
(3)
after applying the 1d duality moves discussed in section
2
. For example, note that for the trivial permutation, the quiver defect reduces to the trivial defect line.

•

For
w\,=\,(1\,2\,3)
.

Here, the permutation
w=(1\,2\,3)
is the identity permutation. The quiver is slightly nontrivial, but the polynomial is trivial. As a warm-up exercise, we will walk through this case systematically, and in later examples, we will only give the highlights.

First, we compute the
{\rm r}_{i,j}
matrix. For this permutation,
w_{0}w=(3\,2\,1)
,
and one quickly finds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm r}_{1,1}^{w_{0}w}&\,=\,\#\,\{\,\ell\,\leq\,1\,|\,(w_{0}w)(\ell)\,\leq\,1\,\}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\\
{\rm r}_{1,2}^{w_{0}w}&\,=\,\#\,\{\,\ell\,\leq\,1\,|\,(w_{0}w)(\ell)\,\leq\,2\,\}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\\
{\rm r}_{2,1}^{w_{0}w}&\,=\,\#\,\{\,\ell\,\leq\,2\,|\,(w_{0}w)(\ell)\,\leq\,1\,\}\,=\,0\penalty\ ,\\
{\rm r}_{2,2}^{w_{0}w}&\,=\,\#\,\{\,\ell\,\leq\,2\,|\,(w_{0}w)(\ell)\,\leq\,2\,\}\,=\,1\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(4.36)

thus recovering the quiver in figure
6
.

From equation (
4.33
), the measure is given in this case by:

\displaystyle({\rm dM})

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{2}\,\prod_{j=1}^{2}\,\left(\,\Delta^{(i,j)}(z)\,\frac{1}{{\rm r}_{i,j}!}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\left(\,\frac{-1}{2\,\pi\,i}\,\right)\,\left(\,\frac{dz_{\alpha}^{(i,j)}}{z_{\alpha}^{(i,j)}}\,\right)\right)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\Delta^{(1,1)}(z)\,\Delta^{(1,2)}(z)\,\Delta^{(2,1)}(z)\,\Delta^{(2,2)}(z)\,\left(\,-\,\frac{1}{2\,\pi\,i}\,\frac{dz_{1}^{(2,2)}}{z_{1}^{(2,2)}}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

with, from (
4.34
),

\Delta^{(1,1)}(z)\,=\,1\,=\,\Delta^{(1,2)}(z)\,=\,\Delta^{(2,1)}(z)\,=\,\Delta^{(2,2)}(z)\penalty\ .

(4.37)

From (
4.35
),

\displaystyle Z^{\rm hor}_{\rm chiral}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z_{1}^{(2,2)}}{x_{1}^{(2)}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z_{1}^{(2,2)}}{x_{2}^{(2)}}\,\right)^{-1}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle Z^{\rm red}_{\rm Fermi}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z_{1}^{(2,2)}}{y_{1}}\,\right)\penalty\ .

For notational simplicity, define
z\,\equiv\,z_{1}^{(2,2)}
, then our index for the case
w=(1\,2\,3)
is:

\mathcal{I}^{\rm(1d)}_{(1\,2\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,-\,\frac{1}{2\,\pi\,i}\,\oint_{\rm JK}\frac{dz}{z}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z}{x_{1}^{(2)}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z}{x_{2}^{(2)}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{z}{y_{1}}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

and the contour integral encloses the poles at
z\,=\,x_{1,2}^{(2)}
, but not the pole at
z=0
.
It is straightforward to evaluate the contour integral to find:

\mathcal{I}^{\rm(1d)}_{(1\,2\,3)}(x,y)\>=\>1\penalty\ .

(4.38)

•

For
w\,=\,(2\,1\,3)
.
In this case, we have the following integrand:

\frac{\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x_{1}^{(2)}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x_{2}^{(2)}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\right)}{\left(1\,-\,\frac{z_{1}^{(1,2)}}{x_{1}^{(1)}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{2}}\right)}\penalty\ .

Computing the corresponding residue integrals, we get the following polynomial:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 1d)}_{(2\,1\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{I}_{(2\,1\,3)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y;q)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\penalty\ .

(4.39)

Here, ‘
\implies
’ means that the we used (
3.19
) to get the quantum version
\mathcal{I}_{(2,1,3)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y;q)
.

•

For
w\,=\,(1\,3\,2)
.
In this case, the integrand is of the form:

\frac{\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{y_{2}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{2}}\right)}\penalty\ .

Taking the residue integrals for this expression, we get:

\mathcal{I}_{(1\,3\,2)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 1d)}_{(1\,3\,2)}(x,y;q)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.40)

In the last equality here, we used the second relation in (
3.20
).

•

For
w\,=\,(2\,3\,1)
.
In this case, the 1d quiver has the following contributions:

\frac{\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{2}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{2}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{y_{2}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{a}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}{y_{1}}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{(1)}_{1}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}\right)\prod_{a,b=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}{x^{(2)}_{b}}\right)}\penalty\ .

The associated residue integrals yield the following polynomial:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 1d)}_{(2\,3\,1)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.41)

Using the two relations in (
3.20
), we see that this index can be written as:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 1d)}_{(2\,3\,1)}(x,y;q)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.42)

•

For
w\,=\,(3\,1\,2)
.
