We have the following integrand:

\frac{\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{y_{2}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{2}_{a}}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,1)}_{1}}{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{(1)}_{1}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{a}}\right)}\penalty\ .

Doing the residue integral for this, we get the following polynomial:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{(3\,1\,2)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}}{y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{1}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(4.43)

Using both (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), we obtain:

\mathcal{I}_{(3\,1\,2)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y;q)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ .

(4.44)

•

For
w\,=\,(3\,2\,1)
.
In this case, the integrand has the following form:

\frac{\left(1-\frac{z_{2}^{(2,1)}}{z_{2}^{(2,2)}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z_{2}^{(2,2)}}{z_{2}^{(2,1)}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{(2)}_{a}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}{y_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{y_{2}}\right)}{\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,1)}_{1}}{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{(1)}_{1}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}\right)\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,1)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}\right)\prod_{b=1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}{x^{(2)}_{b}}\right)}\penalty\ .

Computing the associated residue integral, we get:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{(3\,2\,1)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y)\,&=\,1\,-\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}}{y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{1}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\\
&\quad+\,\frac{x^{(2)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{x^{(1)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{1}\,x^{(2)}_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&=\left(1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}^{(1)}}{y_{2}}\right)\,\left(1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}^{(2)}}{y_{1}}\right)\,\left(1\,-\,\frac{x_{2}^{(2)}}{y_{2}}\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(4.45)

This indeed matches with
(
ahkmox
, Theorem 1.2)
upon identifying the parameters
\epsilon_{i}
in that reference with our 2d twisted masses.
7
7

7

As another explicit example, see the 1d index for
w_{0}\,=\,(\,4\,3\,\,2\,1\,)\in S_{4}
in the corresponding table in appendix
C
.

As before, using the relations (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), we get:

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{(3\,2\,1)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y;q)\,&=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\\
&\quad\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x^{2}_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(4.46)

Note that these 1d indices match exactly with the corresponding quantum Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(q)}(x,y)
listed in (
A.37
). Moreover, the polynomial expression for the longest permutation (
4.46
) can be factorized as:

\mathcal{I}_{(3\,2\,1)}^{(\rm 1d)}(x,y;q)\,=\,\left(1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\right)\left(1\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{\left(1\,-\,q_{1}\right)\,y_{1}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}\,+\,x_{2}}{y_{1}}\right)\penalty\ .

(4.47)

This indeed matches with (
A.30
).

In appendix
C
, we work the
4!=24
1d indices for the case
n=4
— the complete flag manifold Fl
(4)
— and show that they match with the corresponding quantum double Grothendieck polynomials.

Conjecture I:
For the general Fl
(n)
case, we conjecture that the formula (
4.32
) is a JK residue representation of the quantum Grothendieck polynomials. Namely:

\mathcal{I}_{w}^{({\rm 1d})}(x,y;q)\,=\,\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(q)}(x,y)\penalty\ ,

(4.48)

for any
w\in S_{n}
, where it is understood here that the equality is upon using the relations (
3.18
) to transform
\mathcal{I}_{w}^{({\rm 1d})}(x,y)
into
\mathcal{I}_{w}^{({\rm 1d})}(x,y;q)
.

4.4.1
Index calculation for the reduced quivers

For completeness, in this subsection, we exhibit by examples how the dualities discussed in section
2
can speed up the calculation of the 1d indices. For that goal, let us look again at the example of Fl
(3)
. The result of applying these dualities to the six possible line defects is shown in figure
7
. Let us consider the following three cases in more detail:

•

For
w\,=\,(1\,2\,3)
.
As expected from our calculations for this case around (
4.38
), upon using the duality moves, the Schubert defect trivializes, as one can see in figure
7
. And indeed, the 1d Witten index in this case is equal to
1
.

•

For
w\,=\,(2\,1\,3)
.

For this reduced quiver, we have the following simpler integrand:

\left(1\,-\,\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{y_{1}}\right)\left(1\,-\,\frac{z^{(1,2)}_{1}}{x^{(1)}_{1}}\right)^{-1}.

The residue computation is much simpler but reproduces the same result as in (
4.39
).

•

For
w\,=\,(2\,3\,1)
.

This 1d reduced quiver gives the following integrand:

\left(1\,-\,\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}{z^{(2,2)}_{2}}\right)\left(1\,-\,\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{2}}{z^{(2,2)}_{1}}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{2}\left(1\,-\,\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}{y_{1}}\right)\prod_{a,b=1}^{2}\left(1\,-\,\frac{z^{(2,2)}_{a}}{x^{(2)}_{b}}\right)^{-1}\penalty\ .

This much simpler residue yields the same result as in (
4.41
).

5
Schubert point defects in the 2d GLSM

In the previous section, we gave a UV construction of line defects in the 3d GLSM that, in the IR, flow to the Schubert classes in QK
T
(Fl
(n)
). In this section, we discuss the cohomological limit of this construction. That is, we view the quiver in figure
3
as a 0d-2d coupled system and propose that the corresponding half-BPS point defects flow to the Schubert classes in QH
{}^{\bullet}_{T}
(Fl
(n)
). In what follows, we will refer to these defects as
Schubert point defects
.

For a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, the Poincaré dual to the Schubert variety
[X_{w}]
is
\Omega_{w}\in{\rm H}^{2\ell(w)}({\rm Fl}(n))
. In the
T
-equivariant case, this cohomology class is written in terms of the quantum double Schubert polynomial
\mathfrak{S}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)

fomin1997quantum
;
ciocan1997quantum
;
kirillov2000quantum
. For definition and examples, see appendix
A.1
.

Viewing the quiver defect as a 0d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric matrix model (SMM), we will argue in examples that its partition function is none other than the corresponding quantum Schubert polynomial. Like in the 3d case, we conjecture that this matching holds in full generality.

5.1
Partition functions of the matrix model

In the 2d limit
\beta\rightarrow 0
, the figure
3
is now interpreted as a 0d
\mathcal{N}=2
SMM with gauge group
G_{\rm 0d}=\times_{i,j}\,U({\rm r}_{i,j})
and bifundamental chiral and Fermi matter multiplets as discussed above. Following
Franco:2016tcm
;
Closset:2017yte
;
Closset:2023bdr
, the partition function of this quiver SMM can be written in the following JK residue form:

\mathcal{I}_{w}^{({\rm 0d})}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\oint_{\rm JK}\;(dM)\;Z_{\rm chiral}^{\rm vec}\;Z_{\rm chiral}^{\rm hor}\;Z_{\rm Fermi}^{\rm black}\;Z_{\rm Fermi}^{\rm red}\penalty\ .

(5.1)

In the integrand, we have the contributions coming from the bifundamental matter multiplets:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm ver}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\prod_{\beta=1}^{{\rm r}_{i+1,j}}\,\left(\,{u^{(i,j)}_{\alpha}}-{u^{(i+1,j)}_{\beta}}\,\right)^{-1}\penalty\ ,\\
&{Z}_{\rm chiral}^{\rm hor}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,n-1}}\,\prod_{a=1}^{i}\,\left(\,{u_{\alpha}^{(i,n-1)}}-{\widetilde{\sigma}_{a}^{(i)}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-2}\,\prod_{\beta=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\prod_{\gamma=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j+1}}\,\left(\,{u^{(i,j)}_{\beta}}-{u^{(i,j+1)}_{\gamma}}\,\right)^{-1}\,\right]\penalty\ ,\\
&{Z}^{\rm black}_{\rm Fermi}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\,\left[\,\prod_{\gamma=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,n-1}}\prod_{a=1}^{i+1}\left(\,{u^{(i,n-1)}_{\gamma}}-{\widetilde{\sigma}_{a}^{(i+1)}}\,\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\prod_{\beta=1}^{{\rm r}_{i+1,j+1}}\,\left(\,{u^{(i,j)}_{\alpha}}-{u_{\beta}^{(i+1,j+1)}}\,\right)\,\right]\,\penalty\ ,\\
&{Z}^{\rm red}_{\rm Fermi}\,:=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{n-1,i}}\,\left(\,{u^{(n-1,i)}_{\alpha}}-{m_{n-i}}\,\right)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(5.2)

As for the measure
(dM)
in (
5.1
), it is given more explicitly by:

(dM)\,=\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,\Delta^{(i,j)}_{\rm 0d}(u)\,\frac{1}{{\rm r}_{i,j}\,!}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\frac{du_{\alpha}^{(i,j)}}{2\pi i}\,\right]\penalty\ ,

(5.3)

with
\Delta_{\rm 0d}^{(i,j)}
being the Vandermonde determinant factor:

\Delta_{\rm 0d}^{(i,j)}(u)\,:=\,\prod_{1\leq\alpha\neq\beta\leq{\rm r}_{i,j}}\,\left(u^{(i,j)}_{\alpha}-u_{\beta}^{(i,j)}\right)\penalty\ .

(5.4)

Note that the poles in (
5.1
) come from the chiral matter fields, and the JK prescription in this case is simply to consider the contributions coming from all of them.

Analogously to the remarks we made in the Schubert line defects case in subsection
4.2.1
, to obtain the final expression
Z_{\rm Fermi}^{\rm red}(\sigma,m)
, one needs to cancel the contributions coming from the massive chiral fields with their massive Fermi counterparts in the last row in figure
3
. Additionally, to match with the quantum double Schubert polynomials discussed in appendix
A.1
, we use the quantum relations (
3.12
) after performing the residue calculation.
8
8

8

Similarly to the comment we made earlier for the Schubert line defects, using the classical cohomology relations (
3.8
) rather than the quantum ones, we obtain the corresponding double Schubert polynomials which are the Poincaré duals to the Schubert classes in the classical cohomology ring of Fl
(n)
. For definitions and examples, see appendix
A.1
.

5.2
Example: Schubert defects for Fl
(3)

As a first example, let us consider the case with
n=3
. The quiver defects for each one of the six permutations in
S_{3}
are given in figure
6
. From these, one can compute the corresponding partition function. Let us go through these six cases:

•

For
w\,=\,(1\,2\,3)
.
Following our discussion around (
4.36
), and using the general form (
5.1
), we see that, in this case, the 0d partition function is:

\mathcal{I}_{(1\,2\,3)}^{(\rm 0d)}(\widetilde{\sigma},m)\,=\,\oint\frac{du}{2\pi i}\,\frac{u\,-\,m_{1}}{(\,u\,-\,\sigma_{1}^{(2)}\,)\,(\,u\,-\,\sigma_{2}^{(2)}\,)}\,=\,\frac{\sigma_{1}^{(2)}\,-\,m_{1}}{\sigma_{1}^{(2)}\,-\,\sigma_{2}^{(2)}}\,+\,\frac{\sigma_{2}^{(2)}\,-\,m_{1}}{\sigma_{2}^{(2)}\,-\,\sigma_{1}^{(2)}}\,=\,1\penalty\ .

(5.5)

where, for brevity, we introduced
u\equiv u_{1}^{(2,2)}
. The two terms appearing in the second equality are the contributions of the two simple poles in
u
coming from the 1d bifundamental chiral multiplet.

•

For
w\,=\,(2\,1\,3)
.

The integrand in this case is:

\frac{(u_{1}^{(2,2)}-m_{1})\prod_{a=1}^{2}(u_{1}^{(1,2)}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{a}^{(2)})}{(u_{1}^{(1,2)}-u_{1}^{(2,2)})(u_{1}^{(1,2)}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(1)})\prod_{b=1}^{2}(u_{1}^{(2,2)}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{b}^{(2)})}\penalty\ .

Performing the residue integral, we get:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{(2\,1\,3)}(\widetilde{\sigma},m)\,=\,\widetilde{\sigma}^{(1)}_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{(2\,1\,3)}(\sigma,m;\mathsf{q})\,=\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\penalty\ .

(5.6)

To obtain the second result, one also needs to use the relation (
3.9
).

•

For
w\,=\,(1\,3\,2)
.
In this case, the integrand is of the form:

\frac{(u_{1}^{(2,1)}-m_{1})(u_{1}^{(2,2)}-m_{2})}{(u_{1}^{(2,1)}-u_{1}^{(2,2)})(u_{1}^{(2,2)}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(2)})(u_{1}^{(2,2)}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}^{(2)})}\penalty\ .

Similarly to the above, this residue can be computed easily. Upon using the first relation in (
3.13
), one obtains:

\mathcal{I}_{(1\,3\,2)}^{(\rm 0d)}({\sigma},m;\mathsf{q})\,=\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\penalty\ .

(5.7)

•

For
w\,=\,(2\,3\,1)
.
In this case, we have that the integrand of the 0d partition function is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{\rm 0d}^{(2,2)}(u)\,(u^{(2,1)}_{1}-m_{2})\prod_{a=1}^{2}(u^{(2,2)}_{1}-m_{1})\prod_{b=1}^{2}(u^{(1,2)}_{1}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)}_{b})}{(u^{(1,2)}_{1}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{(1)}_{1})\prod_{a=1}^{2}(u^{(2,1)}_{1}-u^{(2,2)}_{a})\prod_{a=1}^{2}(u_{1}^{(1,2)}-u_{a}^{(2,2)})\prod_{a,b=1}^{2}(u^{(2,2)}_{a}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)}_{b})}\penalty\ .

Taking the residue of this, we obtain the following form for the 0d index:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{(2\,3\,1)}(\widetilde{\sigma},m)\,=\,(\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(2)}\,-\,m_{1})\,(\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}^{(2)}\,-\,m_{1})\penalty\ .

(5.8)

Using the two relations in (
3.13
), we obtain:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{(2\,3\,1)}(\sigma,m;\mathsf{q})\,=\,(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1})(\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1})\,-\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ ,

(5.9)

•

For
w\,=\,(3\,1\,2)
.

The partition function for this quiver gauge theory has the following integrand:

\frac{(u_{1}-u_{4})(u_{4}-m_{1})(u_{3}-m_{2})\prod_{a=1}^{2}(u_{2}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{a}^{(2)})}{(u_{1}-u_{2})(u_{1}-u_{3})(u_{3}-u_{4})(u_{2}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(1)})(u_{2}-u_{4})\prod_{a=1}^{2}(u_{4}-\widetilde{\sigma}_{a}^{(2)})}\penalty\ ,

where here, for compactness, we use the notation:

u_{1}\equiv u_{1}^{(1,1)}\penalty\ ,\quad u_{2}\equiv u_{1}^{(1,2)}\penalty\ ,\quad u_{3}\equiv u_{1}^{(2,1)}\penalty\ ,\quad u_{4}\equiv u_{1}^{(2,2)}\penalty\ .

(5.10)

The residue integral with this integrand can be performed recursively, starting with
u_{1}
and ending with
u_{4}
. Doing so, one obtains:

\mathcal{I}_{(3\,1\,2)}^{(\rm 0d)}(\widetilde{\sigma},m)\,=\,-\,\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(2)}\,\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}^{(2)}\,+\,\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(1)}\,(\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}^{(2)}\,+\,\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}^{(2)}\,-\,m_{1}\,-\,m_{2})\,+\,m_{1}\,m_{2}\penalty\ ,

(5.11)

which, upon using (
3.9
) and (
3.13
), gives us:

\mathcal{I}_{(3\,1\,2)}^{(\rm 0d)}(\sigma,m;\mathsf{q})\,=\,(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1})(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2})\,-\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ .

(5.12)

•

For
w\,=\,(3\,2\,1)
. In this case, the integrand has the following form:

{\frac{\Delta_{\rm 0d}^{(2,2)}(u)\,(u_{3}-m_{2})\prod_{a=4}^{5}(u_{a}-m_{1})(u_{1}-u_{a})(u_{2}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)}_{a})}{(u_{1}-u_{2})(u_{1}-u_{3})(u_{2}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{(1)}_{1})\prod_{a=4}^{5}(u_{2}-u_{a})(u_{3}-u_{a})\prod_{b=1}^{2}(u_{a}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)}_{b})}}\penalty\ .

Here, we used the notation (
5.10
) along with
u_{5}\equiv u_{2}^{(2,2)}
.

Computing the corresponding residue, we obtain the following expression for the 0d partition function:

\mathcal{I}_{(3\,2\,1)}^{(\rm 0d)}(\widetilde{\sigma},m)\,=\,(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(1)}_{1}\,-\,m_{2})(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)}_{1}\,-\,m_{1})(\widetilde{\sigma}^{(2)}_{2}\,-\,m_{1})\penalty\ .

(5.13)

Upon using the quantum cohomology relations (
3.9
) and (
3.13
), one finds that the above expressions can be written as follows:

\mathcal{I}_{(3\,2\,1)}^{(\rm 0d)}(\sigma,m;\mathsf{q})\,=\,(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1})(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2})(\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1})\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,\left(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\right)\penalty\ .

(5.14)

Note that, after using the quantum ring relations, these partition functions match exactly with the corresponding quantum double Schubert polynomials
\mathfrak{S}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)
listed in (
A.19
).

As another example, in appendix
C
we work out the partition functions of the SMM defects in Fl
(4)
and check that they match with the quantum double Schubert polynomials upon using the quantum relations (
3.9
) and (
3.14
).

Conjecture II:
We conjecture that, for any
w\in S_{n}
, the 0d partition function (
5.1
) is a residue formula representation for the quantum double Schubert polynomial. Namely:

\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{w}(\sigma,m;\mathsf{q})\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ .

(5.15)

It is understood here that
\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{w}(\sigma,m;\mathsf{q})
is the final form of
\mathcal{I}^{(\rm 0d)}_{w}(\sigma,m)
upon using the quantum relations (
3.12
).

6
Conclusions

In this work, we studied 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauged linear sigma models on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{1}_{\beta}
whose target space is the complete flag manifold
X=
Fl
(n)
. We proposed a description of half-BPS line operators wrapping
{S}_{\beta}^{1}
in terms of 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric quiver gauge theories. We argued that, in the IR, these line defects flow to the Schubert classes in the quantum K-theory ring of Fl
(n)
. We provided two strong pieces of evidence for our proposal:

•

Solving the 1d vacuum equations, we find that they restrict the target
X
, at the point of insertion of the line, to a particular Schubert variety
X_{w}\subseteq X
indexed by the permutation
w
in terms of which the quiver quantum mechanical system is defined. Furthermore, we found that the 1d-3d system actually engineers a resolution
\widetilde{X}_{w}\rightarrow X_{w}
of the Schubert variety.

•

Computing the 1d index of the quiver gauge theories indexed by
w\in S_{n}
, we argued that one obtains the quantum Grothendieck polynomials
\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{w}(x,y)
, which are none other than the Chern characters of the structure sheaf classes
[\mathcal{O}_{w}]\in{\rm QK}(X)
of
X_{w}
.

Taking the small circle limit
\beta\rightarrow 0
, we also proposed a coupled 0d-2d system, where now the bulk theory is the 2d GLSM with target
X
and the point defect is defined in terms of a
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric quiver matrix model. We showed that, computing the 0d partition function, one obtains the quantum Schubert polynomials
\mathfrak{S}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)
. These represent the Schubert classes
[X_{w}]\in{\rm QH}^{\bullet}(X)
.

In the companion paper
Closset:2025pfm
, we generalize this construction of Schubert line defects to arbitrary partial flag manifolds. More generally, it would be very interesting to construct even more general line defects that would directly engineer arbitrary coherent sheaves over some target space
X
of a general 3d GLSM. In addition, already in the case of the complete flag manifold
X={\rm Fl}(X)
, it would be interesting to compute the quantum K-theory ring coefficients in (
2.1
) directly in the Schubert line basis, using the 3-point functions in the 3d
A
-model. While the necessary physics formalism is well developed
Closset:2023bdr
, carrying out such computations efficiently for
n>4
might require clever tricks or advances in computational algebraic geometry. We leave these questions as challenges for future work.

Appendix A
Schubert classes for complete flag manifolds

In this appendix, we review the (equivariant) cohomological and K-theoretical Schubert classes in the complete flag variety Fl
(n)
. These are represented, respectively, by the (double) Schubert and Grothendieck polynomials. Moreover, we discuss the quantum version of these classes, which are represented by the quantum double Schubert polynomials in the quantum cohomology case, and by the quantum double Grothendieck polynomials in the quantum K-theory case.

A comment on permutations.
Let us recall here that a special type of permutations are the
transpositions

s_{ij}
. These exchange the elements at positions
i
and
j
while keeping the other elements fixed. For the case where
i
and
j
are consecutive, we denote the transposition by
s_{i}\equiv s_{i,i+1}
. The permutation group
S_{n}
is generated by these transpositions. A reduced word of the permutation
w\in S_{n}
is of the form
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell(w)}}
, where
\ell(w)
is the length of the permutation. The length can also be defined as

\ell(w)\,:=\,\#\{\,i\,<\,j\,:\,w(i)\,>\,w(j)\,\}\penalty\ .

(A.1)

In particular, we have
\ell({\rm id})=0
for the identity permutation
{\rm id}=(\,1\,2\,\cdots\,n\,)
and
\ell(w_{0})=n(n-1)/2
for the longest permutation
w_{0}=(\,n\,\cdots\,2\,1\,)
.

A.1
Schubert classes in the cohomology of the complete flag

In this section, we review the Chern characters associated with the Schubert classes in the (quantum) cohomology of the complete flag manifold Fl
(n)
. These are called the (quantum) double Schubert polynomials, which are polynomials in the Chern roots of the universal quotient bundles
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
and in the equivariant parameters of the
T=U(1)^{n-1}\subset SU(n)
torus action. As discussed in the bulk of this paper, we denote the equivariant parameters by
m_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
for
i=1,\cdots,n
with the constraint that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}=0
. From the 2d perspective, these correspond to twisted masses for the fundamental matter multiplets
\phi_{n-1}^{n}
— see the discussion around figure
2
for more details.

To define these polynomials, let us start by defining the so-called
divided difference operators

\partial_{i}
associated with transposition
s_{i}
as follows
lascoux1982structure
:

\partial^{(\sigma)}_{i}\,f(\sigma,m)\,=\,\frac{f(\sigma,m)\,-s_{i}^{(\sigma)}\,\cdot\,f(\sigma,m)}{\sigma_{i}\,-\,\sigma_{i+1}}\penalty\ ,

(A.2)

for some polynomial
f(\sigma,m)
. It is understood here that the superscripts indicate with respect to which of the variables (
\sigma
or
m
) we are deriving. The action of the transposition in the numerator is understood as follows:

s_{i}^{(\sigma)}\,\cdot\,f(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1},\cdots,\sigma_{n},m)\,=\,f(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i},\cdots,\sigma_{n},m)\penalty\ .

(A.3)

That is, we swap
\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma_{i+1}
.
For a permutation
w\in S_{n}
with a reduced word
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell(w)}}
,
the associated divided difference operator
\partial_{w}
is defined as:

\partial_{w}\,\equiv\,\partial_{i_{1}}\,\cdots\,\partial_{i_{\ell(w)}}\penalty\ .

(A.4)

A.1.1
Double Schubert polynomials

Now we come to defining the
double Schubert polynomial

\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\sigma,m)

lascoux1982structure
;
LS82b
;
MacDonald1991NotesOS
;
Fulton92
;
lascoux2006symmetry
. For the longest permutation
w_{0}\in S_{n}
,
i.e.

w_{0}=(n\;\cdots\;2\;1)
, we have:

\mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\det\,\left(\,D_{j}\,-\,m_{n-j}\,\mathbb{I}_{j}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

(A.5)

where, the matrix
D_{n}
is defined as the
n\times n
matrix with
\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{n}
along the diagonal and
-1
on the upper-diagonal.
D_{j}
is the upper-left
j\times j
sub-matrix of
D_{n}
. Expanding the determinant factor in (
A.5
), we find that:

\mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{i=1}^{j}\,\left(\,\sigma_{i}\,-\,m_{n-j}\,\right)\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,\sum_{i=0}^{j}\,(-1)^{j-i}\,m_{n-j}^{j-i}\,e_{i}^{j}(\sigma)\,\right]\penalty\ ,

(A.6)

where

e_{i}^{j}(\sigma)\,:=\,e_{i}(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{j})\penalty\ ,

(A.7)

is the
i
-th symmetric polynomial in the
j
variables
\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{j}
.
For a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, we define the corresponding double Schubert polynomial as:

\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(-1)^{\ell(ww_{0})}\,\partial_{ww_{0}}^{(m)}\,\mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\partial_{w^{-1}w_{0}}^{(\sigma)}\,\mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ .

(A.8)

Example I: double Schubert polynomials in
S_{3}
.
Let us take the case
n=3
. In this case, we have
6
possible permutations, and the corresponding double Schubert polynomials, apart from the one associated with the trivial permutation, have the following explicit form:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,1\,3)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(1\,3\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,3\,1)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,1\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,2\,1)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(A.9)

Example II: double Schubert polynomials in
S_{4}
.
For the case
n=4
we have
24
possible permutations. For instance, for four randomly selected permutations, we find:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{S}_{(1\,2\,4\,3)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\sigma_{2}\,+\,\sigma_{3}\,-\,m_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,-\,m_{3}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,1\,4\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{2}\,+\,\sigma_{3}\,-\,m_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,1\,2\,4)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{2}\,+\,\sigma_{3}\,-\,m_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,4\,1\,3)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{2}\,-\,m_{3}\,)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,3\,1\,4)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\,(\,\sigma_{2}\,-\,m_{1}\,)\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.10)

Geometrically, the double Schubert polynomial
\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\sigma,m)
is known to represent the Schubert class
[X_{w}]\in{\rm H}^{\bullet}_{T}({\rm Fl}(n);\mathbb{Z})
in the classical
T
-equivariant cohomology ring of the complete flag Fl
(n)
.

A.1.2
Quantum double Schubert polynomials

Let us now review the quantum version of (
A.8
): the
quantum double Schubert polynomial

\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{w}(\sigma,m)

kirillov2000quantum
;
fomin1997quantum
;
LS14
associated with a permutation
w\in S_{n}
. To define this, let us first define the quantum version of (
A.5
) for the longest permutation. We have:

\mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\det\,\left(\,D^{(\mathsf{q})}_{j}\,-\,m_{n-j}\,\mathbb{I}_{j}\,\right)\penalty\ ,

(A.11)

where the matrix
D^{(\mathsf{q})}_{j}
is the upper-left
j\times j
submatrix of the
n\times n
matrix
D^{(\mathsf{q})}_{n}
whose entries are:

(D^{(\mathsf{q})}_{n})_{i,j}\,=\,\sigma_{i}\,\delta_{i,j}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{j}\,\delta_{i,j+1}\,-\,\delta_{i+1,j}\penalty\ ,\qquad i,j\,=\,1,\cdots,n\penalty\ .

(A.12)

One can easily see that setting the quantum parameters
\mathsf{q}_{i}
to zero recovers the classical case:

\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q}=0)}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ .

(A.13)

Similarly to (
A.6
), one can expand the determinant factor in (
A.11
) as:

\det\,\left(\,D_{j}^{(\mathsf{q})}\,-\,m_{n-j}\,\mathbb{I}_{j}\,\right)\,=\,\sum_{i=0}^{j}\,(-1)^{j-i}\,m_{n-j}^{j-i}\,{\rm E}_{i}^{j}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\penalty\ ,

(A.14)

where we introduced the
quantum elementary symmetric polynomials

{\rm E}_{i}^{j}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})
, which are usually defined as the coefficients of the expansion of the above determinant
fomin1997quantum
. These polynomials have the obvious property that they reduce to the symmetric polynomials (
A.7
) in the classical limit:

{\rm E}_{i}^{j}(\sigma;\mathsf{q}=0)\,=\,e_{i}^{j}(\sigma)\penalty\ .

(A.15)

Example III: Quantum symmetric polynomials for
n=4
.
As an example of these polynomials, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm E}_{1}^{4}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,=\,e_{1}^{4}(\sigma)\penalty\ ,\\
&{\rm E}^{4}_{2}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,=\,e_{2}^{4}(\sigma)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{3}\penalty\ ,\\
&{\rm E}_{3}^{4}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,=\,e_{3}^{4}(\sigma)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{3}\,\sigma_{1}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{3}\,\sigma_{2}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,\sigma_{3}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,\sigma_{4}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,\sigma_{4}\penalty\ ,\\
&{\rm E}_{4}^{4}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,=\,e_{4}^{4}(\sigma)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{3}\,\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{4}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,\sigma_{3}\,\sigma_{4}\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,\mathsf{q}_{3}\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(A.16)

Looking at the form of the determinant
D^{(\mathsf{q})}_{j}
in (
A.12
), it is not difficult to deduce that the quantum elementary polynomials satisfy the following recurrence relation:

{\rm E}_{i}^{j}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,=\,{\rm E}_{i}^{j-1}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,+\,\sigma_{j}\,{\rm E}_{i-1}^{j-1}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{j-1}\,{\rm E}_{i-2}^{j-2}(\sigma;\mathsf{q})\penalty\ .

(A.17)

Note the similarity between these equations and the quantum relations (
3.12
).

Given (
A.11
), one defines the quantum double Schubert polynomial for a permutation
w\in S_{n}
as follows
ciocan1997quantum
;
kirillov2000quantum
:

{\mathfrak{S}}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)\,=\,(-1)^{\ell(ww_{0})}\,\partial^{(m)}_{ww_{0}}\,\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{w_{0}}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ .

(A.18)

Example IV: quantum double Schubert polynomials for
S_{3}
.
As we did for the double Schubert case in (
A.9
) for
n=3
, we have the following quantum version:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(1\,3\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(1\,3\,2)}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(2\,1\,3)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,1\,3)}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(2\,3\,1)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,3\,1)}(\sigma,m)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(3\,1\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,1\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,-\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(3\,2\,1)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,2\,1)}(\sigma,m)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{2}\,)\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(A.19)

Example V: quantum double Schubert polynomials for
S_{4}
.
Let us consider the case with
n=4
, for the same four random permutations as in (
A.10
). One finds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(1\,2\,4\,3)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(1\,2\,4\,3)}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(3\,1\,4\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,1\,4\,2)}(\sigma,m)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,(\,\sigma_{1}\,-\,\sigma_{3}\,)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{(3\,1\,2\,4)}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(3\,1\,2\,4)}(\sigma,m)\,-\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,4\,1\,3)}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,4\,1\,3)}(\sigma,m)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,(\sigma_{1}\,+\,\sigma_{2})\,-\,\mathsf{q}_{2}\,(\sigma_{1}\,-\,m_{1})\,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\,m_{2}\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\,-\,m_{3}\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,3\,1\,4)}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{(2\,3\,1\,4)}(\sigma,m)\,+\,\mathsf{q}_{1}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.20)

In the classical limit, these clearly reduce to the double Schubert polynomials in (
A.10
).

Geometric interpretation.
From a geometric point of view,
the quantum double Schubert polynomial
\mathfrak{S}^{(\mathsf{q})}_{w}(\sigma,m)
represents the Schubert class of
[X_{w}]\in{\rm QH}^{\bullet}_{T}({\rm Fl}(n);\mathbb{Z})
in the
T
-equivariant quantum cohomology ring of Fl
(n)
.

A.2
Schubert classes in the K-theory of the complete flag

In this subsection, we uplift the Schubert polynomials discussed above from the cohomology to the K-theory. In this case, the Schubert classes
[\mathcal{O}_{w}]
will be the (quantum) K-theory classes of the structure sheaves of the Schubert variety
X_{w}
. The relevant polynomials will be the equivariant Chern characters of these sheaves. Recall that the equivariant Chern character provides a map:

{\rm ch}_{T}\,:\,{\rm K}_{T}(X)\,\longrightarrow\,{\rm H}^{\bullet}_{T}(X)\penalty\ .

(A.21)

To define these polynomials, we start by defining the
Demazure operator
(or isobaric divided difference operator). For a transposition
s_{i}\in S_{n}
, we denote the corresponding Demazure operator by
\pi_{i}
and we define it explicitly as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\pi^{(x)}_{i}\,:=\,1\,-\,x_{i}\,\partial^{(x)}_{i}\,=\,\frac{x_{i+1}\,-\,x_{i}\,s^{(x)}_{i}}{x_{i+1}\,-\,x_{i}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\pi^{(y)}_{i}\,:=\,1\,+\,y_{i+1}\,\partial_{i}^{(y)}\,=\,\frac{y_{i}\,-\,y_{i+1}\,s^{(y)}_{i}}{y_{i}\,-\,y_{i+1}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.22)

For a permutation
w\in S_{n}
with a reduced word
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell(w)}}
, we define:

\pi^{(x)}_{w}\,:=\,\pi^{(x)}_{i_{1}}\,\cdots\,\pi^{(x)}_{i_{\ell(w)}}\penalty\ ,\qquad\qquad\pi^{(y)}_{w}\,:=\,\pi^{(y)}_{i_{1}}\,\cdots\,\pi^{(y)}_{i_{\ell(w)}}\penalty\ .

(A.23)

A comment on the physics conventions.
Note that, in the mathematical literature, the Demazure operator with respect to a variable
z
is usually defined as:

\pi_{i}^{(z)}\,:=\,\partial_{i}^{(z)}\,\left(\,1\,-\,z_{i+1}\,\right)\penalty\ .

(A.24)

To compare with our QFT calculations in the main text of this paper, we are here defining the operators
\pi_{i}^{(x)}
and
\pi^{(y)}_{i}
in (
A.22
) in terms of the ‘shifted Wilson line operators’
Gu:2022yvj

z_{i}=1-x_{i}
or
z_{i}=1-y_{i}^{-1}
, respectively.

A.2.1
Double Grothendieck polynomials

To define the double Grothendieck polynomial for a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, we start by defining the double Grothendieck polynomial associated with the longest permutation
w_{0}\in S_{n}
as
(
lascoux1982structure
, (2.1))
(see also
(
lenart2006quantum
, section 8)
):

\mathfrak{G}_{w_{0}}(x,y)\,:=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\prod_{i=1}^{j}\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{i}}{y_{n-j}}\,\right)\,=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,1\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{j}\,\frac{(-1)^{i}}{y^{i}_{n-j}}\,e_{i}^{j}(x)\,\right]\penalty\ .

(A.25)

The polynomials
e_{i}^{j}(x)
are defined in (
A.7
). For a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, we define the associated double Grothendieck polynomial as:

\mathfrak{G}_{w}(x,y)\,:=\,\pi^{(y)}_{ww_{0}}\,\mathfrak{G}_{w_{0}}(x,y)\,=\,\pi^{(x)}_{w^{-1}w_{0}}\,\mathfrak{G}_{w_{0}}(x,y)\penalty\ .

(A.26)

Example VIII: double Grothendieck polynomials in
S_{3}
.
For
n=3
, we have the following list of double Grothendieck polynomials:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{G}_{(2\,1\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(1\,3\,2)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(2\,3\,1)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(3\,1\,2)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(3\,2\,1)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(A.27)

Example IX: double Grothendieck polynomials in
S_{4}
.
Let us now take
n=4
. From the definition (
A.26
) we find the following polynomials:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{G}_{(1\,2\,4\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(3\,1\,4\,2)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}^{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(3\,1\,2\,4)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(2\,4\,1\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{2}\,y_{3}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}_{(2\,3\,1\,4)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ .\end{split}

(A.28)

Geometric interpretation.
From a geometric point of view, the double Grothendieck polynomials are known to represent the equivariant Chern characters of the Schubert classes
[\mathcal{O}_{w}]\in{\rm K}_{T}(\text{Fl}(n))
, see
e.g.

FL94
.

2d limit and cohomology classes.

To reduce these polynomials to their cohomological counterparts, recall the definition of the K-theoretic roots
x_{i}=e^{-\beta\sigma_{i}}
for the quotient bundle and
y_{i}=e^{-\beta m_{i}}
for the weights of the
U(1)^{n-1}
torus action. At the level of the double Grothendieck polynomial (
A.26
), we find that:

\lim_{\beta\rightarrow 0}\,\left(\,\beta^{-\ell(w)}\,\mathfrak{G}_{w}\left(e^{-\beta\sigma_{i}},e^{-\beta m_{i}}\right)\,\right)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ .

(A.29)

As an example of this, one can indeed check that, in this limit, the polynomials (
A.27
) reduce to (
A.9
), and the polynomials (
A.28
) reduce to (
A.10
).

A.2.2
Quantum double Grothendieck polynomials

Motivated by the form of the double Grothendieck polynomial in (
A.25
), and following the steps of
lenart2006quantum
, let us ‘‘quantize’’ it in the following way:
9
9

9

Our convention for the quantum Grothendieck polynomials differs slightly from that of
lenart2006quantum
(see Example 3.19 there).
Starting from our variables, one may perform the substitution
x_{i}\,\mapsto\,(1\,-\,q_{i})\,(1\,-\,x_{i})
and
y_{i}\,\mapsto\,(1\,-\,y_{i})^{-1}
to match their expressions. Our notation appears more natural from the point of view of the 3d GLSM.

\mathfrak{G}_{w_{0}}^{(q)}(x,y)\,:=\,\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\left[\,1\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{j}\,\frac{(-1)^{i}}{y^{i}_{n-j}}\,{\rm F}_{i}^{j}(x;q)\,\right]\penalty\ ,

(A.30)

where
{\rm F}_{i}^{j}
are the quantum Lenart–Maeno (LM) polynomials defined explicitly as:

{\rm F}_{i}^{j}(x;q)\,:=\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[j]\\
|I|=i\end{subarray}}\,\left[\,\prod_{k\in I}\,{x_{k}}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in I\\
k+1\in I\end{subarray}}\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{k}}\,\right]\penalty\ ,

(A.31)

where here
[j]:=\{1,2,\cdots,j\}
. In the classical limit
q_{i}\rightarrow 0
, this reduces to the elementary symmetric polynomials
e_{i}^{j}(x)
that we used in defining the double Grothendieck polynomials (
A.5
).
With the expression (
A.30
) in hand, we define the quantum double Grothendieck polynomial
\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{w}(x,y)
for any permutation
w\in S_{n}
as follows:

\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(q)}(x,y)\,:=\,\pi_{ww_{0}}^{(y)}\,\mathfrak{G}_{w_{0}}^{(q)}(x,y)\penalty\ .

(A.32)

Similar to the symmetric polynomials that we studied earlier in this appendix, the LM polynomials (
A.31
) can be generated using the following
j\times j
matrix:

[{\rm G}_{j}(x;q)]_{k,l}\,=\,\begin{cases}x_{k}\,\prod_{s=l}^{k-1}\,q_{s}\,x_{s}\penalty\ ,\qquad&l\,<\,k\penalty\ ,\\
x_{k}\penalty\ ,\qquad&l\,=\,k\penalty\ ,\\
-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{k}}\penalty\ ,\qquad&l\,=\,k\,+\,1\penalty\ ,\\
0\penalty\ ,\qquad&{\rm otherwise}\penalty\ ,\end{cases}

(A.33)

for
k,l=1,\cdots,j
. For example, for
j=4
, the
4\times 4
generating matrix G
4
has the following explicit form:

{\rm G}_{4}(x;q)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}x_{1}&\penalty\ \penalty\ -\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ 0&\penalty\ \penalty\ 0\\
q_{1}\,x_{1}\,x_{2}&\penalty\ \penalty\ x_{2}&\penalty\ \penalty\ -\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{2}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ 0\\
q_{1}\,q_{2}\,x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}&\penalty\ \penalty\ q_{2}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}&\penalty\ \penalty\ x_{3}&\penalty\ \penalty\ -\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{3}}\\
q_{1}\,q_{2}\,q_{3}\,x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}\,x_{4}&\penalty\ \penalty\ q_{2}\,q_{3}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}\,x_{4}&\penalty\ \penalty\ q_{3}\,x_{3}\,x_{4}&\penalty\ \penalty\ x_{4}\\
\end{array}\right)\penalty\ .

(A.34)

Indeed, one can easily check that:

\det\,\left(\,{\rm G}_{j}(x;q)\,-\,\lambda\,\mathbb{I}_{j\times j}\,\right)\,=\,\sum_{i=0}^{j}\,(-1)^{j-i}\,\lambda^{j-i}\,{\rm F}_{i}^{j}(x;q)\penalty\ .

(A.35)

Moreover, we observe that, in the classical
q_{i}\rightarrow 0
limit, the matrix (
A.33
) reduces to the matrix
D_{j}
appearing in (
A.5
).

Similarly to the recurrence relations (
A.17
), one can show that the LM polynomials F
{}_{i}^{j}(x;q)
satisfy the following recurrence relation:

{\rm F}_{i}^{j}(x;q)\,=\,{\rm F}_{i}^{j-1}(x;q)\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{j-1}}\,x_{j}\,{\rm F}_{i-1}^{j-1}(x;q)\,-\,\frac{q_{j-1}}{1\,-\,q_{j-1}}\,x_{j}\,{\rm F}_{i-1}^{j-2}(x;q)\penalty\ .

(A.36)

Note that this recurrence relation is similar to the QK relations of Fl
(n)
(
3.18
). This has to do with the fact that these polynomials form a basis of the QK
({\rm Fl}(n))

lenart2006quantum
.

Example X: quantum double Grothendieck polynomials in
S_{3}
.
For
n=3
, we have the following quantum double Grothendieck polynomials:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(2\,1\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,\mathfrak{G}_{(2\,1\,3)}(x,y)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(1\,3\,2)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}},\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(2\,3\,1)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(3\,1\,2)}(x,y)\,=\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,\right)\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,\right)\,-\,\frac{q_{1}}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}},\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(3\,2\,1)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}}\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(A.37)

In the limit
q_{i}\rightarrow 0
, these reduce to the corresponding double Grothendieck polynomials.

Example XI: quantum double Grothendieck polynomials in
S_{4}
.
Let us look at the case of
n=4
. We have the following quantum Grothendieck polynomials for some of the possible permutations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(1\,2\,4\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{1}{(1\,-\,q_{1})(1\,-\,q_{2})}\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(3\,1\,4\,2)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{2}}\frac{x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}}\,+\,\frac{1}{(1\,-\,q_{1})(1\,-\,q_{2})}\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\,\frac{1}{(1\,-\,q_{1})(1\,-\,q_{2})}\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(1\,-\,q_{1})(1\,-\,q_{2})}\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}^{2}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(3\,1\,2\,4)}(x,y)\,=\,\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(3\,1\,2)}(x,y)\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(2\,4\,1\,3)}(x,y)\,=\,1\,-\,\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\,-\,\frac{x_{2}}{y_{1}}\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}}\,-\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}}{y_{2}\,y_{3}}\,+\,\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{1\,-\,q_{1}}\,\frac{x_{1}\,x_{2}^{2}}{y_{1}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}-\frac{1}{(1\,-\,q_{1})^{2}}\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}}{y_{1}^{2}\,y_{2}\,y_{3}}\penalty\ ,\\
&\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(2\,3\,1\,4)}(x,y)\,=\,\mathfrak{G}_{(2\,3\,1)}(x,y)\penalty\ ,\end{split}

(A.38)

where,
\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(3\,1\,2)}(x,y)
and
\mathfrak{G}^{(q)}_{(2\,3\,1)}(x,y)
are given in (
A.37
). Clearly, these reduce to (
A.28
) in the classical limit.

Geometric interpretation.
The quantum double Grothendieck polynomials are the
T
-equivariant Chern characters of the Schubert classes
[\mathcal{O}_{w}]
in the
T
-equivariant quantum K-theory ring of the complete flag Fl
(n)
, see
e.g.

lenart2006quantum
.

2d limit and quantum cohomology.
Similarly to what we did for the double Grothendieck polynomials earlier in (
A.29
), let us consider the 2d limit of the quantum double Grothendieck polynomials (
A.32
).
As reviewed in the main text, we need to keep in mind that the quantum cohomology and quantum K-theory deformation parameters
\mathsf{q}_{i}
and
q_{i}
are related via:

{q}_{i}\,\equiv\,\beta^{2}\,\mathsf{q}_{i}\penalty\ ,\qquad i\,=\,1,\cdots,n-1\penalty\ .

(A.39)

Therefore, taking
x_{i}\,\equiv\,e^{-\beta\sigma_{i}}
and
y_{i}\equiv e^{-\beta m_{i}}
, we find the following limit:

\lim_{\beta\rightarrow 0}\,\left(\,\beta^{-\ell(w)}\,\mathfrak{G}^{({q})}_{w}\left(e^{-\beta\sigma_{i}},e^{-\beta m_{i}}\right)\,\right)\,=\,\mathfrak{S}^{({\mathsf{q}})}_{w}(\sigma,m)\penalty\ ,

(A.40)

where the quantum double Schubert polynomial
\mathfrak{S}_{w}^{(\mathsf{q})}(\sigma,m)
is defined in (
A.18
).
For instance, in this limit, (
A.37
) reduces to (
A.19
). Similarly, the polynomials (
A.38
) reduce to (
A.20
).

Appendix B
Resolution of Schubert varieties from mathematics

In this appendix, we explain how the geometry arising from our 1d-3d GLSM corresponds to two known constructions of resolutions of Schubert varieties: via bioriented flags and via quiver Grassmannians.
The relation to bioriented flags is relatively direct, while for quiver Grassmannians we briefly unpack the relevant definitions before showing the equivalence.

B.1
Resolved Schubert variety as a bioriented flag

Following
cibotaru2020bioriented
, the Schubert varieties with respect to a reference flag
E_{\bullet}
, are defined as:

X^{w}(E_{\bullet})\,:=\,\{F_{\bullet}\,\in\,\text{Fl}(n)\,\mid\,\dim(F_{i}\,\cap\,E_{j})\,\geq\,\text{r}_{i,j}^{w}\}\penalty\ ,\quad{\rm r}^{w}_{i,j}\,=\,\#\{\,l\,\leq\,i\mid w(l)\,\leq\,j\,\}\penalty\ .

(B.1)

This definition differs slightly from our convention for
X_{w}
in equation (
4.1
) — the two conventions are equivalent up to relabeling:
X_{w}=X^{w_{0}w}
.
One introduces the product space:

\mathcal{P}^{w}\,:=\,\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\,\text{Gr}({\text{r}_{i,j}^{w}},\mathbb{C}^{n})\penalty\ ,

(B.2)

whose points consist of products of subspaces
F_{i,j}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
of dimension
\text{r}_{i,j}^{w}
. The
bioriented flag
is defined as:

\text{Fl}^{w}:=\{F_{\bullet,\bullet}\in\mathcal{P}^{w}\mid F_{i,j}\subset F_{i,j+1},\penalty\ F_{i,j}\subset F_{i+1,j}\}\penalty\ .

(B.3)

The authors of
cibotaru2020bioriented
further define:

\widehat{X}^{w}(E_{\bullet})\,:=\,\{F_{\bullet,\bullet}\,\in\,\text{Fl}^{w}\,\mid\,F_{n,i}\,=\,E_{i},\penalty\ i\,=\,1,2,\cdots,n\}\penalty\ .

(B.4)

Our physical construction realizes precisely
\widehat{X}^{w_{0}w}(E_{\bullet})
, as is clear from figure
4
. Moreover, it is proved in
cibotaru2020bioriented
that the projection:

\widehat{X}^{w}(E_{\bullet})\to\text{Fl}(n)\penalty\ ,\quad F_{\bullet,\bullet}\mapsto F_{\bullet,n}\penalty\ ,

(B.5)

is a resolution of
X_{w}
, which is isomorphic to the Bott–Samelson resolution of
X_{w}
corresponding to a certain word representation of
w
.

B.2
Resolved Schubert variety as a quiver Grassmannian

In this appendix, we would like to explain why the target space of our 1d-3d GLSM is also precisely a quiver Grassmannian
\text{Gr}_{{\bf r}^{w}}(M)
in the sense of
iezzi2025quivergrassmanniansbottsamelsonresolution
.

To define the quiver Grassmannian, one starts with a specific quiver representation
M
(with relations), then
\text{Gr}_{{\bf r}^{w}}(M)
is the collection of all the quiver subrepresentations
N
of
M
, whose dimension vectors are specified by
{\bf r}^{w}
. For the full definitions and technical details, we refer the reader to
iezzi2025quivergrassmanniansbottsamelsonresolution
.
For our purposes, it will be sufficient to specify the quiver representation
M
directly, without introducing the underlying quiver and its arrows explicitly. The quiver representation
M
is given by:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here each node is a vector space
M_{i,j}=E_{j}
, where
(0\subset E_{1}\subset E_{2}\subset\cdots\subset E_{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n})
forms a fixed full flag in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The horizontal arrows are identity maps, while the vertical arrows are inclusions
\iota_{i+1,i}:E_{i}\hookrightarrow E_{i+1}
.
By construction, all squares in the diagram commute. This commutativity realizes quiver relations that are otherwise specified as part of the quiver data (and follow from the
E
-term equations in our GLSM).

The quiver Grassmannian
\text{Gr}_{{\bf r}^{w}}(M)
consists of subrepresentations
N
of
M
. For each subrepresentation
N
, it consists of the following data:

•

We have subspaces
N_{i,j}\subseteq M_{i,j}=E_{i}
and
\dim(N_{i,j})=\text{r}^{w}_{i,j}
.

•

For each arrow in
M
, the induced arrow in
N
is the unique inclusion map between the corresponding subspaces, of the form
N_{i,j}\hookrightarrow N_{i+1,j}
or
N_{i,j}\hookrightarrow N_{i,j+1}
.

•

The commutativity relation

(N_{i,j}\hookrightarrow N_{i,j+1}\hookrightarrow N_{i+1,j+1})=(N_{i,j}\hookrightarrow N_{i+1,j}\hookrightarrow N_{i+1,j+1})

is trivially satisfied.

Therefore, we have the representation
N
:

Now we can forget about the subrepresentation condition
N_{i,j}\subset E_{i}
, and we only have the condition
\dim(N_{i,j})=\text{r}^{w}_{i,j}
. The commutativity of the newly added squares follows from the fact that all the arrows are inclusions.

The above diagram with the conditions
\dim(N_{i,j})=\text{r}^{w}_{i,j}
is the same as the configuration in figure
4
, up to a rotation of 90 degrees and a relabeling of the permutation. This rotation is due to a difference in conventions: the quantity denoted
\text{r}_{i,j}^{w}
in
iezzi2025quivergrassmanniansbottsamelsonresolution
corresponds to what we denote by
\text{r}_{j,i}^{w}
in equation (
4.4
). The relabeling is due to the fact that we use
\text{r}^{w_{0}w}_{i,j}
instead of
\text{r}^{w}_{i,j}
in the definition of Schubert varieties.

(2\,3\,1\,4)

(3\,1\,2\,4)

(1\,3\,4\,2)

(1\,4\,2\,3)

(3\,2\,4\,1)

(3\,4\,2\,1)

(2\,4\,1\,3)

(3\,1\,4\,2)

1-\left(\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})}{y_{1}}+\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})}{y_{2}}\right)+\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})-e_{2}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}\newline
+\left(\frac{e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}}+\frac{e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}^{2}}\right)-\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}}

(4\,1\,2\,3)

(4\,2\,3\,1)

(1\,4\,3\,2)

1-\frac{e_{2}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}-\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})}{y_{3}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)+\frac{e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)\newline
+\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}-\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}y_{3}}

1-\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{2}}\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{1-q_{1}}+\frac{x_{2}x_{3}}{1-q_{2}}+x_{1}x_{3}\right)-\frac{x_{1}x_{2}(y_{1}+y_{2})}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}+\frac{x_{1}x_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\newline

(3\,4\,1\,2)

1-e_{1}(x^{(2)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)+e_{2}(x^{(2)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}^{2}}\right)\newline
+\frac{e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})-e_{2}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}-\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})-e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)\newline
+\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{2}(x^{(3)})-e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}}

1-\left(x_{1}+x_{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)+\frac{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}{y_{1}y_{2}}+\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{1-q_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}^{2}}\right)+\frac{2x_{1}x_{2}}{y_{1}y_{2}}\newline
-\frac{q_{2}x_{2}x_{3}}{(1-q_{2})y_{1}y_{2}}-\frac{x_{1}x_{2}(y_{1}+y_{2})}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}}\left(x_{1}+x_{2}-\frac{q_{2}x_{3}}{1-q_{2}}\right)\newline
+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{1-q_{1}}-\frac{q_{2}x_{3}}{1-q_{2}}\right)

(4\,2\,1\,3)

1-\frac{e_{1}(x^{(2)})}{y_{1}}-e_{1}(x^{(1)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{3}}\right)+e_{2}(x^{(2)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}}-\frac{1}{y_{2}y_{3}}\right)\newline
+\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(2)})}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3})-\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{2}(x^{(2)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}\left(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}\right)\newline
+\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\left(e_{1}(x^{(3)})e_{2}(x^{(2)})-e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})\right)\newline
+\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\left(e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{2}(x^{(3)})-e_{3}(x^{(3)})\right)\newline
+\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}\left(e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})-e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{2}(x^{(3)})\right)\newline
-\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}\left(e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})-e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})\right)

1-\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{y_{1}}+\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}+\frac{x_{1}}{y_{3}}\right)+\left(x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{3}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}y_{3}}\right)\newline
+\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{1-q_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}}-\frac{1}{y_{2}y_{3}}\right)-\frac{(1-q_{1})x_{1}^{3}+(1-2q_{1})x_{1}^{2}x_{2}-q_{1}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\newline
-\frac{q_{1}q_{2}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}-\frac{(y_{2}+y_{3})x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}+\frac{x_{1}^{3}x_{2}}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}-\frac{q_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}}{(1-q_{1})^{2}y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}

(4\,1\,3\,2)

1-e_{1}(x^{(1)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{3}}\right)+\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})-e_{2}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}\newline
+\left(e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(2)})-e_{2}(x^{(2)})\right)\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{3}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}y_{3}}\right)\newline

+\frac{\left(e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{2}(x^{(3)})-e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})+e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})\right)}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\newline
+\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})-e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}y_{3}}

1-\left(\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}+\frac{x_{1}}{y_{2}}+\frac{x_{1}}{y_{3}}\right)+\frac{x_{1}(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3})}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\left(x_{1}-\frac{q_{1}x_{2}}{1-q_{1}}\right)-\frac{x_{2}x_{3}}{(1-q_{2})y_{1}y_{2}}\newline
-\frac{x_{1}^{3}}{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}+\frac{q_{1}\left(2x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\right)}{(1-q_{1})y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}+\frac{(1-q_{1}q_{2})x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}}{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}\newline
-\frac{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}(x_{1}-y_{1}-y_{2})}{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}(x_{1}-y_{1}-y_{2})}{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}y_{3}}-\frac{q_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}}{(1-q_{1})^{2}(1-q_{2})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}y_{3}}

(4\,3\,1\,2)

\left(1-\frac{e_{1}(x^{(1)})}{y_{3}}\right)\newline
\times\left(1-e_{1}(x^{(2)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)+e_{2}(x^{(2)})\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}^{2}}\right)\right.\newline
+\left.\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})+e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})-e_{2}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}y_{2}}-\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{1}(x^{(3)})}{y_{2}y_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)\right.\newline
\left.+\frac{e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{2}y_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)+\frac{e_{2}(x^{(2)})e_{2}(x^{(3)})-e_{1}(x^{(2)})e_{3}(x^{(3)})}{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}}\right)

\left(1-\frac{x_{1}}{y_{3}}\right)\newline
